The dressing field method for diffeomorphisms: a relational framework
J. T. François André

Abstract
The dressing field method is a tool to reduce gauge symmetries. Here we extend it to cover the case of diffeomorphisms. The resulting framework is a systematic scheme to produce Diff ⁡ ( M ) \Diff(M) -invariant objects, which has a natural relational interpretation. Its precise formulation relies on a clear understanding of the bundle geometry of field space. By detailing it, among other things we stress the geometric nature of field-independent and field-dependent diffeomorphisms, and highlight that the heuristic “extended bracket” for field-dependent vector fields often featuring in the covariant phase space literature can be understood as arising from the Frölicher-Nijenhuis bracket. Furthermore, by articulating this bundle geometry with the covariant phase space approach, we give a streamlined account of the elementary objects of the (pre)symplectic structure of a Diff ⁡ ( M ) \Diff(M) -theory: Noether charges and their bracket, as induced by the standard prescription for the presymplectic potential and 2-form. We give conceptually transparent expressions allowing to read the integrability conditions and the circumstances under which the bracket of charge is Lie, and the resulting Poisson algebras of charges are central extensions of the Lie algebras of field-independent ( 𝔡 ​ 𝔦 ​ 𝔣 ​ 𝔣 ​ ( M ) \mathfrak{diff}(M) ) and field-dependent vector fields. We show that, applying the dressing field method, one obtains a Diff ⁡ ( M ) \Diff(M) -invariant and manifestly relational formulation of a general relativistic field theory. Relying on results just mentioned, we easily derive the “dressed” (relational) presymplectic structure of the theory. This reproduces or extends results from the gravitational edge modes and gravitational dressings literature. In addition to simplified technical derivations, the conceptual clarity of the framework supplies several insights and allows us to dispel misconceptions.

.

Keywords
: Relationality, bundle geometry, covariant phase space, gravitational dressings, edge modes.

1
Introduction

The dressing field method (DFM) is an algorithm to build basic forms on a bundle, i.e. to obtain gauge-invariants in gauge theories. It was first introduced systematically in
[
GaugeInvCompFields
;
Francois2014
;
Attard_et_al2017
]
, and its implications regarding the philosophy of gauge theories has been first expounded in
[
Francois2018
]
.
Its applications range from the construction of twistors and tractors in conformal Cartan geometry
[
Attard-Francois2016_II
;
Attard-Francois2016_I
]
, to reformulation of electroweak physics dispensing with the notion of spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB)
[
Berghofer-et-al2023
]
.
More recently, it was shown in
[
Francois2021
;
Francois-et-al2021
]
that it is the geometric underpinning of the notion of
edge modes
introduced in recent years in the study of the presymplectic structure of gauge theories over bounded regions
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
;
Geiller2017
;
Speranza2018
;
Geiller2018
;
Geiller2019
;
Freidel-et-al2020-1
;
Freidel-et-al2020-2
;
Freidel-et-al2020-3
;
Teh-et-al2021
]
– which was first hinted at in
[
Teh-et-al2020
]
.

The DFM has been first developed for theories with internal gauge symmetries, i.e. Yang-Mills theories and gauge theories of gravity formulated via Cartan geometry. The interested reader can find self-contained expositions in sections 3 and 4.3.1 of
[
Francois2021
]
, section 2.3 of
[
Francois-et-al2021
]
, or chapter 5 of
[
Berghofer-et-al2023
]
. See also
[
Riello-Schiavina2023b
;
Riello-Schiavina2023a
]
for another mathematical development and
applications.
Here, we aim to provide a detailed exposition of its natural extension to theories with
\Diff(M)
-symmetry, and to show how the resulting framework encompasses and unifies a diversity of notions in old and new literature on gravity (to be named below).
The proper mathematical formulation of the DFM requires some familiarity with the geometry of the field space
\Phi
of
\Diff(M)
-theories.
The paper is thus organised as follows.

In section
2
, in a systematic manner, we lay the elementary notions pertaining to the geometry of
\Phi
as an infinite dimensional fiber
bundle
with structure group
\Diff(M)
.
First, we will be interested in the bundle structure of
\Phi
: the smooth action of
\Diff(M)
,
the definition of its group of vertical diffeomorphisms (otherwise known as “transformations under
field-dependent
gauge parameters”), giving in particular the geometric definition of gauge transformations.
Then, we consider the differential structure: tangent bundle and remarkable vector fields, the de Rham complex, remarkable spaces of differential forms, and the graded algebra of their derivations.

This will be the occasion to show that the heuristic bracket for field-dependent gauge parameters (vector fields) introduced in
[
Bergmann-Komar1972
]
and
[
Salisbury-Sundermeyer1983
]
, then again in
[
Barnich-Troessaert2009
]
– and often used in covariant phase space literature, see e.g.
[
Gomes-et-al2018
;
Freidel-et-al2021
;
Freidel-et-al2021bis
;
Chandrasekaran-et-al2022
]
– is but a special case of the Frölicher-Nijenhuis bracket for vector-valued differential forms on
\Phi
. The field space Lie derivative along such field-dependent vector fields is simply the Nijenhuis-Lie derivative
[
Kolar-Michor-Slovak
]
. Of course, both these facts are true when an internal gauge group is taken as structure group of
\Phi
instead of
\Diff(M)
.

Once these elementary notions properly introduced, we will mention two natural notions of connections
\Phi
can be endowed with: Ehresmann connections, and a generalisation known as “twisted connections”
[
Francois2019_II
]
.
The latter are
directly relevant to understanding the geometry behind classical and/or quantum
\Diff(M)
-anomalies.

Finally, we will give an account of integration on
M
as a natural pairing operation on a space we call the
associated bundle of regions
: a fiber bundle associated to field space via the defining representation of the structure group
\Diff(M)
, i.e. the field of open sets
U\subset M
. This allows us to show how field-dependent diffeomorphisms act (non-trivially) on integrals.

Section
3
details the dressing field method (DFM) for diffeomorphisms.
It is a systematic procedure to produce
basic
forms on
\Phi
, invariant under vertical diffeomorphisms, provided one can identify or build a
dressing field
. A notion we define precisely.
We consider the question of residual symmetries, arising in case of partial symmetry reduction, or of possible ambiguities in the choice of dressing field. We show that in the latter case, the space of dressed fields has its own bundle structure, mirroring that of
\Phi
.
The notion of dressed integrals is then defined, and we discuss its relevance to physical applications (to be detailed later), especially regarding the variational principle.

The conceptual implications of the DFM are discussed, and contact with the literature is made.
It is argued that the framework developed, technically implementing Einstein’s point coincidence argument to field theory
[
Norton1993
;
Stachel2014
;
Giovanelli2021
]
, has a natural relational interpretation.

We then aim to showcase a natural application of the DFM to the covariant presymplectic structure of
\Diff(M)
-theories, to highlight how it reproduces results
pertaining to this literature.

In section
4
, we thus give our account of covariant phase space methods for
\Diff(M)
-theories, making explicit use of the bundle geometry of
\Phi
exposed in
2
. Given a choice of Lagrangian, we define Noether currents and charges for both field-independent and field-dependent vector fields, and show that, equipped with the bracket induced by the presymplectic 2-form, their Poisson algebras are respectively central extensions of the Lie algebras of the structure group of field space,
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
, and of the group of vertical diffeomorphisms of field space. We give (hopefully) conceptually transparent and concrete expressions for all relevant objects.

Then we move to deriving geometrically the vertical transformations of key objects: Lagrangian, field equations, presymplectic potential and 2-form.
This will show how and why
\Diff(M)
-theories enjoy a much larger “covariance group” under field-dependent diffeomorphisms, as hinted at in
[
Bergmann-Komar1972
]
.
This is used to derive naturally the previously mentioned Noether charges for field-dependent parameters together with their bracket.

Our goal here is twofold. Firstly, we aim to be both synthetic and systematic in our presentation of elementary covariant phase space notions:
by streamlining their technical derivation and improving the conceptual clarity, we hope to lower the cost of entry for newcomers (PhDs and postdocs most notably, whose time is precious).
Secondly, as stated above, we will rely on the derived results to showcase the DFM.

This we do in a final short section
, where the DFM is applied to easily write down the dressed version of a
\Diff(M)
-theory together with their
basic
presymplectic structure.
This will make manifest that the DFM is the unifying geometric framework underpinning various notions featuring in the literature on gravity: Notably in the past decade that of
gravitational edge modes
as introduced in
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]

and developed e.g. in
[
Speranza2018
;
Freidel-et-al2020-1
;
Freidel-et-al2020-2
;
Freidel-et-al2020-3
;
Chandrasekaran_Speranza2021
;
Speranza2022
;
Chandrasekaran-et-al2022
]
,
and of
gravitational dressings
as proposed in
[
Giddings-Donnelly2016
;
Giddings-Donnelly2016-bis
;
Giddings-Kinsella2018
;
Giddings2019
;
Giddings-Weinberg2020
]
– see also
[
Kabel-Wieland2022
]
– as well as the more recent idea of “dynamical reference frames” as expounded in
[
carrozza-et-al2022
;
Hoehn-et-al2022
]
, or yet that of “embedding maps/fields” as advocated in
[
Speranza2019
;
Ciambelli-et-al2022
;
Speranza2022
;
Ciambelli2023
]
.

But a more fundamental fact, we will argue again, is that the DFM makes manifest the
relational
character of general relativistic physics:
By systematically implementing a notion of
relational observables

[
Tamborino2012
]
, or
\Diff(M)
-
Dirac variables
,
it allows to reformulate a general relativistic field theory in a
\Diff(M)
-invariant and
manifestly relational
way.
This provides some conceptual insights. In particular it dispels the often encountered notion that “boundaries breaks
\Diff(M)
-invariance” (a statement equivalent to the famous hole argument). It will also suggest a resolution as to why general relativistic theories could be successfully experimentally tested before solving the issue of their observables. The technical relational formulation provided by the DFM encompassed various notions of “scalar coordinatisation” proposed for GR, e.g.
[
Rovelli1991
;
Brown-Kuchar1995
;
Rovelli2002b
;
Pons-et-al2009
;
Pons-et-al2009b
]
.

We conclude by taking stock of our main results and hint at future applications and developments. Technical appendices complete the main text.

2
Geometry of field space

In this section we give a sense of the bundle geometry of field space as an infinite dimensional manifold.
We aim for a correct conceptual description rather than for a perfectly rigorous account, we thus refer to the the relevant literature, e.g.
[
Frolicher-Kriegl1988
;
Kriegl-Michor1997
]
, to back the soundness of extending standard notions defined in the finite dimensional context to the infinite dimensional setting.

2.1
Field space as a principal bundle

As we are interested in
\Diff(M)
-theories, our field space is
\Phi=\Gamma(\mathcal{T})
where
\mathcal{T}
denotes collectively any kind of tensor or pseudo-tensor bundles. Generically these are vector and affine bundles associated to the frame bundle
LM
of
M
, and, if we are interested in gauge field theories based on a principal bundle
P(M,H)
with structure group
H
, it may include the affine bundle of (Ehresmann or Cartan) connections
\mathcal{C}
and/or any vector bundles
E
associated to
P
via representations of
H
. If we have furthermore a
S\!O
-reduction
OM
of
LM
, we may consider also spinor bundles
\mathsf{S}
associated to
OM
via spin representations, and a fortiori the bundles
\mathsf{S}\otimes E
whose sections would describe gauge-charged spinors. A set of sections of these bundles will be noted collectively as
\phi\in\Phi
, i.e. a single point in field space.

The group
\Diff(M)
has a natural action by pullback on sections of those bundles:
Given
\phi\in\Phi
and
\psi\in\Diff(M)
we have,
\phi^{\psi}:=\psi^{*}\phi
– where the left-hand side is a notation for the pullback on the right-hand side. We will use it when convenient.
Due to the well-known relation
(f\circ g)^{*}=g^{*}\circ f^{*}
for any two smooth maps
M\xrightarrow{g}N\xrightarrow{f}Q
, this action is a right-action.
1
1

1

This is clear on forms, a.k.a. contravariant tensors. On vector fields and more general covariant tensors, the pullback action is
\psi^{*}\vcentcolon={\psi^{-1}}_{*}
, the pushforward by the inverse diffeomorphism. So, on general (mixed) tensors,
\psi^{*}
is indeed a well-defined right action of
\Diff(M)
.

We therefore note,

\displaystyle\Phi\times\Diff(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Phi,

(1)

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto R_{\psi}\phi:=\psi^{*}\phi,

with indeed, for another
\psi^{\prime}\in\Diff(M)
:
R_{\psi^{\prime}}R_{\psi}\phi:={\psi^{\prime}}^{*}\psi^{*}\phi=(\psi\circ\psi^{\prime})^{*}\phi=:R_{\psi\circ\psi^{\prime}}\phi
. The field space is fibered by the action of
\Diff(M)
, the fiber through a point
\phi
being its orbit
\mathcal{O}(\phi)
under diffeomorphisms. The set of orbits, or moduli space, we denote
\Phi/\Diff(M)=:\mathcal{M}
. Under adequate restrictions of either of
\Phi
or
\Diff(M)
, this means that the field space
\Phi
can be understood as an infinite dimensional principal fiber bundle over
\mathcal{M}
, its
base
space, with structure group
\Diff(M)
.
2
2

2

We refer to
[
Kriegl-Michor1997
]
for rigorous results on how to describe a fibered space with the non-compact structure group
\Diff(M)
.
3
3

3

In particular, this requires that all points
\phi
have trivial stability groups, meaning that we a priori reject Killing symmetries of
\phi
.
We note,

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{M},

(2)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi(\phi)=:[\phi]

The projection
\pi
of course satisfies the usual relation
\pi\circ R_{\psi}=\pi
.
The fiber over a point
[\phi]\in\mathcal{M}
can be noted
\pi^{-1}([\phi])=\mathcal{O}(\phi)
, and it is diffeomorphic to the structure group
\Diff(M)
as a manifold.

As a fiber bundle,
\Phi
is locally trivialisable. Meaning, for any open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
there is a morphism of
\Diff(M)
-spaces
t:\Phi_{|\mathcal{U}}\mapsto\mathcal{U}\times\Diff(M)
,
\phi\mapsto(\pi(\phi),\uppsi(\phi))
, called a
trivialisation
. In others words, it is a local coordinate system on
\Phi
, and
\uppsi
is the fiber coordinate of
\phi
. It is s.t.
t(\phi^{\psi})=R_{\psi}\,t(\phi)=t(\phi)^{\psi}=(\pi(\phi),\uppsi(\phi)^{\psi})
. A trivialisation is equivalent to the datum of a
local section
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\Phi_{|\mathcal{U}}
,
[\phi]\mapsto\boldsymbol{\sigma}([\phi])=\vcentcolon\phi_{0}
, s.t.
\pi\circ\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\text{id}_{\mathcal{U}}
. One considers that
t(\phi_{0})=([\phi],\text{id})
, so that any other point
\phi
of the fiber over
[\phi]
can be reached via the action of the structure group:
\phi=R_{\uppsi}\boldsymbol{\sigma}([\phi])=\boldsymbol{\sigma}([\phi])^{\uppsi}
.
Unless
\Phi
is trivial, there are no global section
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\Phi
. A choice of local section
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is a
gauge fixing
– a choice of representative in each gauge orbit over
\mathcal{U}
– and the previous statement simply expresses the inexistence of a globally valid gauge fixing procedure, i.e. the Gribov-Singer obstruction
[
Gribov
;
Singer1978
]
.

Two choices of local sections
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
are related via
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=R_{\boldsymbol{g}}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, where
\boldsymbol{g}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\Diff(M)
,
[\phi]\mapsto\boldsymbol{g}([\phi])
, belongs to the set of
transition functions
of
\Phi
.
4
4

4

The latter constitute
passive
gauge transformations, or
gluings
, on
\mathcal{M}
. We will be concerned throughout this paper with
active
gauge transformations associated to vertical automorphisms on
\Phi
(and more generally with vertical diffeomorphisms of
\Phi
). Conceptually, these two are in the same relationship as coordinate changes, a.k.a. passive diffeomorphisms, and
\Diff(M)
, a.k.a active diffeomorphisms.

We stress that this means that a choice of gauge-fixing/local section
cannot be invariant
under a further action of the structure group
\Diff(M)
.

2.1.1
Natural transformation groups

As an infinite dimensional manifold
\Phi
has a diffeomorphism group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)
, but
as a principal bundle
its maximal transformation group is its group of automorphisms
\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi):=\big\{\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)\,|\,\Xi\circ R_{\psi}=R_{\psi}\circ\Xi\big\}
. Its elements preserve the fibration structure, and thus project naturally as elements of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\mathcal{M})
.

The subgroup of
vertical
diffeomorphisms
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\vcentcolon=\left\{\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)\,|\,\pi\circ\Xi=\pi\right\}
induce the identity transformation on
\mathcal{M}
:
These are moving along fibers, therefore to
\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
corresponds a unique
\boldsymbol{\psi}:\Phi\rightarrow\Diff(M)
s.t.
\Xi(\phi)=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}\phi:=[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)]^{*}\phi
. In other words
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
. It should be noticed that the map composition law for
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
gives rise to a peculiar composition operation for
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
.
Indeed, for
\Xi,\Xi^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
to which correspond
\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
, one has:

\Xi^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\circ}\,\!\Xi\,(\phi)=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\left(\Xi(\phi)\right)}\,\Xi(\phi)=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\left(\Xi(\phi)\right)}\,R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}\phi=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\left(\Xi(\phi)\right)}\phi.

Thus,

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\circ}\,\!\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\ \ \text{ corresponds to }\ \ \boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\circ}R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big)\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big).

(3)

Notice we distinguish the composition law
\boldsymbol{\circ}
of maps on
\Phi
, from the composition law
\circ
of maps on
M
.

This is an example of the composition law of the infinite dimensional group of bisections of a Lie groupoid
[
Mackenzie2005
;
Schmeding-Wockel2015
;
Maujouy2022
]
. As a mater of fact, what we have been discussing above can be reframed in the groupoid framework as follows:
One defines the generalised action groupoid
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\rightrightarrows\Phi
with
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\Phi\rtimes\Big(C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\Big)
, and with source and target maps

s:\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\rightarrow\Phi
,
(\phi,\boldsymbol{\psi}\simeq\Xi)\mapsto\phi
, and

t:\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\rightarrow\Phi
,
(\phi,\boldsymbol{\psi}\simeq\Xi)\mapsto\Xi(\phi)=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{*}\phi
.
The associative composition law:
g\circ f\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
for
f,g\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is defined whenever
t(f)=s(g)
.
It generalises the action groupoid
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}=\Phi\rtimes\Diff(M)\rightrightarrows\Phi
associated with the right action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Phi
. The group of bisections
\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is the set of sections of
s
– maps
\sigma:\Phi\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
,
\phi\mapsto\sigma(\phi)=(\phi,\boldsymbol{\psi}\sim\Xi)
s.t.
s\circ\sigma=\text{id}_{\Phi}
– such that
t\circ\sigma:\Phi\rightarrow\Phi
is invertible: thus
t\circ\sigma\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)
. We have indeed, for
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
, that
t\circ\sigma=\Xi\,(=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*})\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
.
The group law of bisections is defined as

\big(\sigma_{2}\star\sigma_{1}\big)(\phi)\vcentcolon=\sigma_{2}\big((t\circ\sigma_{1}(\phi)\big)\circ\sigma_{1}(\phi)
.
After composition with the target map
t
on the left, this reproduces precisely the peculiar group law (
3
).
Then, slightly abusing the terminology, we may refer to
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
as the group of bisections of the generalised action groupoid
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
associated to
\Phi
.
In the rest of this paper though, we will simply, and accurately, refer to it as the group of generating maps of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
.

The latter group generalises the better known, and more often discussed, subgroup of
vertical automorphisms

\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi):=\big\{\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)\,|\,\pi\penalty\ \circ\penalty\ \Xi\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pi\big\}=\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\cap\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)
, isomorphic to the
gauge group

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M):=\big\{\boldsymbol{\psi}:\Phi\rightarrow\Diff(M)\,|\,\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi^{\psi})=\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\circ\psi\big\}

(4)

via
\Xi(\phi)=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}\,\phi
still.
5
5

5

It is indeed easy to check that the equivariance condition on
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
flows from the definition of an automorphism: On the one hand
\Xi\circ R_{\psi}(\phi):=\Xi(\phi^{\psi}):=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi^{\psi})}\phi^{\psi}:=[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi^{\psi})]^{*}\phi^{\psi}=[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi^{\psi})]^{*}\psi^{*}\phi=[\psi\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi^{\psi})]^{*}\phi
. On the other hand
R_{\psi}\circ\Xi(\phi):=R_{\psi}([\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)]^{*}\psi)=\psi^{*}[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)]^{*}\phi=[\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\circ\psi]^{*}\phi
. The equality of both expression imposes:
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi^{\psi})=\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\circ\psi
, or
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\psi
. This condition is thus necessary if a map
\boldsymbol{\psi}
is to induce a vertical automorphism of
\Phi
.

The defining equivariance of elements
\boldsymbol{\psi}
of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
implies that to
\Xi^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\circ}\,\!\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)

corresponds

\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
: i.e. the composition operation
\boldsymbol{\circ}
in
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
translates to the usual (pointwise) composition operation
\circ
of the group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
– as expected and familiar in the Yang-Mills case.

We have that
\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)
is in the normaliser of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
, and as a group is a subgroup of its normaliser we get:
N_{\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)}\big(\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\big)=\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\cup\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)
. As a special case, we have
N_{\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)}\big(\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\big)=\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\triangleleft\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)
, which gives the short exact sequence (SES) of groups characteristic of a principal bundle:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\mathcal{M}),

(5)

where the image of each arrow is in the kernel of the next. We remark that, as physical degrees of freedom are gauge invariant, they should be in the moduli space
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, if anything is deserving of the name of “
physical
symmetries”, it is the right-most group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\mathcal{M})
.

In the physics literature, especially on the covariant phase space approach to gravity theories, one often encounters the notion of “
field-dependent
” gauge transformations, or diffeomorphisms.
We submit that the group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
is the correct mathematical embodiment of this notion. We nonetheless stress that stricto sensu, geometric (active) gauge transformations on
\Phi
are defined by the action of the subgroup
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
. Still, the linearised action of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
gives rise to the so-called “extended bracket” for infinitesimal field-dependent gauge transformations, or diffeomorphisms, as introduced by Bergmann & Komar
[
Bergmann-Komar1972
]
and Salisbury & Sundermeyer
[
Salisbury-Sundermeyer1983
]
, then again by Barnich & Troessaert (BT) in
[
Barnich-Troessaert2009
]
– we will further indicate that this bracket is just the Frölicher-Nijenhuis (FN) bracket of vector-valued differential forms
[
Kolar-Michor-Slovak
]
. Obviously, the structure group
\Diff(M)
supplies the notion of “
field-independent
” gauge transformations or diffeomorphisms.

The linearization of (
5
) is the Atiyah Lie algebroid of the principal bundle
\Phi
. To understand it, and more generally the differential structure of
\Phi
, let us first remind a general result that we will use often.

Consider the manifolds
M
,
N
and their tangent bundles
TM
,
TN
, together with a diffeomorphism
\psi:M\rightarrow N
and the flow
\upphi_{\tau}:N\rightarrow N
of a vector field
X\in\Gamma(TN)
, s.t.
X_{|\upphi_{0}}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\upphi_{\tau}|_{\tau=0}\in T_{\upphi_{0}}N
. One defines the flow

\displaystyle\varphi_{\tau}:=\psi^{-1}\circ\upphi_{\tau}\circ\psi:M\rightarrow M

(6)

of a vector field
Y\in\Gamma(TM)
related to
X
as:

\displaystyle Y:=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\left(\psi^{-1}\circ\upphi_{\tau}\circ\psi\right)\big|_{\tau=0}=(\psi^{-1})_{*}\,X\circ\psi.

(7)

Indeed as maps we have
M\xrightarrow{\psi}N\xrightarrow{X}TN\xrightarrow{(\psi^{-1})_{*}}TM
. Their composition give the above vector field
Y:M\xrightarrow{}TM
.
We say that
Y
and
X
are
\psi
-
related
. It is a standard result that
\psi
-relatedness is a morphism of Lie algebras, that is:
[Y,Y^{\prime}]=(\psi^{-1})_{*}[X,X^{\prime}]\circ\psi
.
We will therefore remember that
X\in\Gamma(TN)
and
Y\in\Gamma(TM)
are
\psi
-related (
7
) when their flows are
\psi
-
conjugated
(
6
).

The following lemma can be considered a generalisation of the above: Suppose
\phi
is some tensor field on
M
, and
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi
its Lie derivative along
X\in\Gamma(TM)
with flow
\varphi_{\tau}
. Then, for
\psi\in\Diff(M)
we have,

\displaystyle\psi^{*}\big(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi\big)

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\varphi^{*}_{\tau}\phi\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}(\varphi_{\tau}\circ\psi)^{*}\phi\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}(\varphi_{\tau}\circ\psi)^{*}\ (\psi^{-1})^{*}\psi^{*}\phi\big|_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}(\psi^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\tau}\circ\psi)^{*}\ \psi^{*}\phi\big|_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle=:\mathfrak{L}_{[(\psi^{-1})_{*}X\circ\psi]}\,(\psi^{*}\phi).

(8)

2.2
Differential structure

As a manifold,
\Phi
has a tangent bundle
T\Phi
, a cotangent bundle
T^{\star}\Phi
, or more generally a space of forms
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
.
We will consider both structures in turn. It will be useful to distinguish easily the pushforward and pullback on
M
and
\Phi
; we thus reserve
*
to denote these notions on
M
, and use
\star
for their counterparts on
\Phi
.

2.2.1
Tangent bundle and subbundles

Sections of the tangent bundle
\mathfrak{X}:\Phi\rightarrow T\Phi
are vector fields on
\Phi
, we note
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
. They form a Lie algebra under the bracket of vector field
[\ ,\,]:\Gamma(T\Phi)\times\Gamma(T\Phi)\rightarrow\Gamma(T\Phi)
.
We may write a vector field at
\phi\in\Phi
as
\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\Psi_{\tau}(\phi)\,\big|_{\tau=0}
, with
\Psi_{\tau}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\Phi)
its flow s.t.
\Psi_{\tau=0}(\phi)=\phi
.
Considered as derivations of the algebra of functions
C^{\infty}(\Phi)
we would write, in analogy with the finite dimensional case:
\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{X}(\phi)\tfrac{\delta}{\delta\phi}
, where
\tfrac{\delta}{\delta\phi}
is of course the functional differentiation w.r.t.
\phi
, and
\mathfrak{X}(\phi)
are the functional components.

The pushforward by the projection is the map
\pi_{\star}:T_{\phi}\Phi\rightarrow T_{\pi(\phi)}\mathcal{M}=T_{[\phi]}\mathcal{M}
.
The pushforward by the right action of
\psi\in\Diff(M)
is the map
R_{\psi\star}:T_{\phi}\Phi\rightarrow T_{\psi^{*}\phi}\Phi
.
In general
R_{\psi\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\neq\mathfrak{X}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}
, meaning that a generic vector field “rotates” as it is pushed vertically along fibers. For this reason, in general
\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}
is not a well-defined vector field on the base
\mathcal{M}
: Indeed, at
[\phi]\in\mathcal{M}
the vector obtained would vary depending on where on the fiber over
[\phi]
the projection is taken.

There is a remarkable Lie subalgebra of
\Gamma(T\Phi)
, the
right-invariant
vector fields:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\text{\!\tiny{inv}}}(T\Phi):=\left\{\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)\,|\,R_{\psi\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=\mathfrak{X}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}\right\}.

(9)

Those do not rotate as they are pushed vertically. Consequently, they project as well-defined vector fields on
\mathcal{M}
:
Indeed, for
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma_{\text{\!\tiny{inv}}}(T\Phi)
, we have
\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}=\pi_{\star}R_{\psi\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=(\pi\circ R_{\psi})_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=:\mathfrak{Y}_{|[\phi]}\in T_{[\phi]}\mathcal{M}
. The result is independent of where on the fiber over
[\phi]
the projection is taken, so
\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}=:\mathfrak{Y}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
is a well defined vector field. Furthermore, the defining property of invariant vector fields implies that their flow is an automorphism of
\Phi
: Indeed we have,

\displaystyle R_{\psi\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\psi}\Psi_{\tau}(\phi)\,\big|_{\tau=0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{X}_{|R_{\psi}\phi}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\Psi_{\tau}(R_{\psi}\phi)\,\big|_{\tau=0}.

(10)

The equality of both implies
R_{\psi}\circ\Psi_{\tau}=\Psi_{\tau}\circ R_{\psi}
. Therefore, the Lie subalgebra of right-invariant vector fields is the Lie algebra of the group
\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi)
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}(\Phi)=\big(\Gamma_{\text{\!\tiny{inv}}}(T\Phi);[\ ,\,]\big).

(11)

The tangent bundle
T\Phi
has a canonical subbundle, the
vertical tangent bundle
defined as
V\Phi:=\ker\pi_{\star}
. In other words, vertical vector fields are elements of
\Gamma(V\Phi):=\left\{\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)\,|\,\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}=0\right\}
. Since
V\Phi
is a subbundle,
\Gamma(V\Phi)
is a Lie subalgebra of
\Gamma(T\Phi)
, even an ideal. Indeed, since
\pi_{\star}:\Gamma(T\Phi)\rightarrow\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
is a Lie algebra morphism, we have, for
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(V\Phi)
and
\mathfrak{Y}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
:
\pi_{\star}[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]=[\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{X},\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{Y}]=[0,\pi_{\star}\mathfrak{Y}]=0
, i.e.
[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]\in\Gamma(V\Phi)
.

Vertical vector fields can be understood as arising from the linearization of the right action of
\Diff(M)
.
Its characterization requires that we describe the Lie algebra of
\Diff(M)
. It is well know that as a vector space it it given by
\Gamma(TM)
, the vector fields on
M
, but as a Lie algebra its Lie bracket is
minus
the bracket of vector fields:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{diff}(M)=\big(\Gamma(TM);-[\ ,\,]\big).

(12)

We may use the notation
[X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}}:=-[X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}}
when useful.

Let us have a look at the types of vertical vector fields induced by the respective actions of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
.

A
fundamental
vertical vector field at
\phi\in\Phi
generated by
X=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\psi_{\tau}\big|_{\tau=0}\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)
with flow
\psi_{\tau}\in\Diff(M)
is:

\displaystyle X^{v}_{|\phi}:=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\psi_{\tau}}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\psi^{*}_{\tau}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=:\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi,

(13)

where
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi
is the spacetime Lie derivative of the field
\phi
along the vector field
X\in T(M)
. The spacetime Lie derivative is also given by the Cartan formula
\mathfrak{L}_{X}=[\iota_{x},d]=\iota_{X}d+d\iota_{X}
, with
d
the de Rham exterior derivative on
M
– It is derivation of degree
0
of the algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
of forms on
M
, since
\iota_{X}
is of degree
-1
and
d
is of degree
1
. Naturally,
X^{v}_{|\phi}
is tangent to the
\Diff(M)
-orbit
\mathcal{O}[\phi]
at
\phi\in\Phi
, hence the name. As expected, it satisfies
\pi_{\star}X^{v}\equiv 0
, since

\pi_{\star}X^{v}_{|\phi}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\pi\circ R_{\psi_{\tau}}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\pi(\phi)\,\big|_{\tau=0}
.
As is standard, one shows (see Appendix
) that the map
|^{v}:\mathfrak{diff}(M)\rightarrow\Gamma(V\Phi)
,
X\mapsto X^{v}
, is a Lie algebra morphism: i.e.
([X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}})^{v}=(-[X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}})^{v}=[X^{v},Y^{v}]
.

The pushforward by the right-action of a fundamental vertical vector field is:

\displaystyle R_{\psi\star}X^{v}_{|\phi}:=

\displaystyle\,\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\psi}\circ R_{\psi_{\tau}}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\psi_{\tau}\circ\psi}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\psi_{\tau}\circ\psi}\,R_{\psi^{-1}\circ\psi}\,\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{(\psi^{-1}\circ\psi_{\tau}\circ\psi)}\,R_{\psi}\,\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{(\psi^{-1}\circ\psi_{\tau}\circ\psi)}\,\psi^{*}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!:\left((\psi^{-1})_{*}\,X\circ\psi\right)^{v}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}.

(14)

Where we use the relation between
\psi
-relatedness of vector fields and
\psi
-conjugation of their flows.
6
6

6

This can be understood as the incarnation of the lemma (
2.1.1
) on field space.
A fundamental vector field generated by the action of the (Lie algebra of the) structure group is thus not right-invariant.

But the fundamental vector fields induced by
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
, the Lie algebra of the gauge group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
, are. For
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
, we have of course
\boldsymbol{X}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}\,\big|_{\tau=0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
. Now, given the definition (
4
) of the gauge group, the transformation property of whose elements can be written
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\psi
– and reminding the link between
\psi
-relatedness of vector fields and
\psi
-conjugation of their flow – the former is,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M):=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{X}:\Phi\rightarrow\Gamma(TM)\ |\ R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{X}=(\psi^{-1})_{*}\,\boldsymbol{X}\circ\psi\,\right\}.

(15)

This transformation property can also be written as:
\boldsymbol{X}(\phi^{\psi})=\boldsymbol{X}(\psi^{*}\phi)=(\psi^{-1})_{*}\,\boldsymbol{X}(\phi)\circ\psi
.
We may remark that the infinitesimal version is given by the Lie derivative on
\Phi
along the corresponding fundamental vector field:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{X}=X^{v}(\boldsymbol{X})=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}\boldsymbol{X}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,(\psi^{-1}_{\tau})_{*}\,\boldsymbol{X}\circ\psi_{\tau}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=:\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{X}=[X,\boldsymbol{X}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}}=[\boldsymbol{X},X]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}}.

(16)

Which is natural, and as one would expect. Now a fundamental vector field generated by
\boldsymbol{X}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
is,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{v}_{|\phi}:=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}(\phi)}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}(\phi))^{*}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=:\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\phi.

(17)

Its pushforward by the right-action of the structure group is:

\displaystyle R_{\psi\star}\boldsymbol{X}^{v}_{|\phi}:=

\displaystyle\,\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\psi}\circ R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}(\phi)}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{(\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}(\phi)\circ\psi)}\,R_{\psi}\,\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}(\psi^{*}\phi)}\,\psi^{*}\phi\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{X}^{v}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}.

(18)

These are right-invariant, as advertised.
Furthermore, one shows (see Appendix
) that the map
|^{v}:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)\rightarrow\Gamma_{\text{\!\tiny{inv}}}(V\Phi)
,
\boldsymbol{X}\mapsto\boldsymbol{X}^{v}
, is a Lie algebra
anti
-morphism: i.e.
([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}})^{v}=(-[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}})^{v}=-[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]
.
Therefore, since the Lie subalgebra of right-invariant vertical vector fields is the Lie algebra of the group
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)=\big(\Gamma_{\text{\!\tiny{inv}}}(V\Phi);[\ ,\,]\big).

(19)

We can thus write the infinitesimal version of (
5
), i.e. the SES describing the Atiyah Lie algebroid of the bundle
\Phi
:

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)\xrightarrow{|^{v}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}(\Phi)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\star}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow 0,

(20)

where again, the “
physical
symmetries” are in the right-most Lie algebra
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(\mathcal{M})
.
7
7

7

A splitting of this SES, i.e. the datum of a map
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}(\Phi)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
– or equivalently of a map
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}(\Phi)
– which would allow to decompose a (right-invariant) vector field on
\Phi
as a sum of a gauge element and a vector field on
\mathcal{M}
, is supplied by a choice of Ehresmann connection
1
-form on
\Phi
. See later.

Finally, consider the Lie algebra of the group of vertical automorphisms
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\vcentcolon=\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{|\phi}^{v}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\tau}(\phi)\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}(\phi)}\phi\big|_{\tau=0}\in\Gamma(V\Phi)\right\},

(21)

where
\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\tau}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
, and
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
is the flow of
\boldsymbol{X}:\Phi\rightarrow\Gamma(TM)\simeq\mathfrak{diff}(M)
.
Therefore, we have that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
.
The pushforward of
\boldsymbol{X}^{v}
by the action of the structure group
\Diff(M)
is the same as for a fundamental vector field (
2.2.1
),

\displaystyle R_{\psi\star}\boldsymbol{X}^{v}_{|\phi}=\left((\psi^{-1})_{*}\,\boldsymbol{X}\circ\psi\right)^{v}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}.

(22)

Now, here the map
|^{v}:C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\rightarrow\Gamma(V\Phi)
is also a Lie algebra morphism, yet the bracket on
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
is not simply the bracket in
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
but an “extended” bracket that take into account the
\Phi
-dependence of its elements.
Indeed, one shows that for
\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
:

\displaystyle\big[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big]

\displaystyle=-[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}}^{v}+[\boldsymbol{X}^{v}(\boldsymbol{Y})]^{v}-[\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}(\boldsymbol{X})]^{v},

\displaystyle=\left\{[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]^{\phantom{-}}_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}+\boldsymbol{X}^{v}(\boldsymbol{Y})-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}(\boldsymbol{X})\right\}^{v}=\vcentcolon\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}.

(23)

The result is proven in the short note
[
Francois2023-b
]
(for the finite dimensional case).
The bracket on
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
is thus,

\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}:=[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]^{\phantom{-}}_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}+\boldsymbol{X}^{v}(\boldsymbol{Y})-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}(\boldsymbol{X}).

(24)

From this follows naturally that,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]=\boldsymbol{L}_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]}=\boldsymbol{L}_{\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}}.

(25)

We may observe that the result (
2.2.1
) reproduces the results of Appendix
:
i.e. on the one hand if
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\rightarrow X,Y\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)
the second and third term of the extended bracket vanish and
[X^{v},Y^{v}]=(-[X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}})^{v}=([X,Y]_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}})^{v}
,
and on the other hand if if
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
we may use (
16
) to obtain the second and third term of the extended bracket so that
[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]=([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}})^{v}=(-[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}})^{v}
.
Naturally, the relation (
2.2.1
) is the infinitesimal counterpart of (
3
), with (
24
) reflecting the peculiar composition law in
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
.

Echoing our discussion of the action groupoid
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, let us briefly recall the Lie algebroid picture of the above
[
Mackenzie2005
;
Crainic-Fernandez2011
]
. Associated to the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
on
\Phi
, i.e. to the Lie algebra morphism
\alpha=|^{v}:\mathfrak{diff}(M)\rightarrow\Gamma(V\Phi)\subset\Gamma(T\Phi)
, is the action (or transformation) Lie algebroid
\boldsymbol{A}=\Phi\rtimes\mathfrak{diff}(M)\rightarrow\Phi
with anchor
\rho:\boldsymbol{A}\rightarrow V\Phi\subset T\Phi
,
(\phi,X)\mapsto\alpha(X)_{|\phi}=X^{v}_{|\phi}
, inducing the Lie algebra morphism
\tilde{\rho}:\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})\rightarrow\Gamma(V\Phi)\subset\Gamma(T\Phi)
.
The space of sections
\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})=\{\Phi\rightarrow\boldsymbol{A},\phi\mapsto\big(\phi,\boldsymbol{X}(\phi)\big)\}
is naturally identified with the space
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
,
8
8

8

Sections of
\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})
are just the graphs of elements of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
.
so that
\tilde{\rho}=\alpha=|^{v}
.
The Lie algebroid bracket
[\ \,,\ ]_{\text{\tiny{$\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})$}}}
is
uniquely determined by the Leibniz condition
[\boldsymbol{X},f\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})$}}}=f[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})$}}}+\tilde{\rho}(\boldsymbol{X})f\cdot\boldsymbol{Y}
, for
f\in C^{\infty}(\Phi)
,
and the requirement that
[\ \,,\ ]_{\text{\tiny{$\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})$}}}=[\ \,,\ ]_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}
on constant sections.
It is found to be:
[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\Gamma(\boldsymbol{A})$}}}=[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}+\tilde{\rho}(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{Y}-\tilde{\rho}(\boldsymbol{Y})\boldsymbol{X}
.
The action Lie algebroid bracket indeed reproduces (
24
) above – which was obtained via a computation which amounts to showing explicitly
that
\tilde{\rho}=|^{v}
is indeed a Lie algebra morphism.

Quite intuitively, the action Lie algebroid
\boldsymbol{A}
is the limit of the action Lie groupoid
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
when the target and source maps are infinitesimally close. Hence
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\Gamma(A)
is the Lie algebra of the group (of bisections)
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
, as we’ve shown explicitly above.

To the best of my knowledge, the bracket (
24
) has been first introduced in physics by
Bergmann & Komar
[
Bergmann-Komar1972
]
(for
\phi=g_{\mu\nu}
) and Salisbury & Sundermeyer
[
Salisbury-Sundermeyer1983
]
in their investigations of the largest possible symmetry group of General Relativity – see eq.(3.1)-(3.2) in
[
Bergmann-Komar1972
]
and eq.(2.1) in
[
Salisbury-Sundermeyer1983
]
. It was latter reintroduced by Barnich & Troessaert in their study
[
Barnich-Troessaert2009
]
of asymptotic symmetries of gravity in the flat limit at null infinity. This Bergmann-Komar-Salisbury-Sundermeyer (BKSS) bracket
\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}_{\text{\tiny{BKSS}}}=-\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}

appears in eq.(8) in
[
Barnich-Troessaert2009
]
and more recently e.g. in eq.(3.3) in
[
Freidel-et-al2021
]
, eq.(2.12) in
[
Freidel-et-al2021bis
]
, eq.(2.21) in
[
Gomes-et-al2018
]
, eq.(2.3) in
[
Chandrasekaran-et-al2022
]
, eq.(1.1) in
[
Speranza2022
]
.
As this references show, this bracket is commonly used in the literature concerned with the covariant symplectic structure of gravity and with the analysis of its asymptotic symmetries (BMS and extensions).
This bracket was first interpreted as a bracket of action Lie algebroid in
[
Barnich2010
]
(eq. (28) – or (3.11) of the preprint).
In addition of showing here explicitly how (
24
) arises simply from standard bundle geometry, we show in the next section below that it is also an instance in degree
0
of the Frölicher-Nijenhuis bracket of vector valued forms.

Finally, let us state an elementary yet important result (proven in Appendix
): The pushforward by a vertical diffeomorphism
\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
, to which corresponds
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
,
is a map
\Xi_{\star}:T_{\phi}\Phi\rightarrow T_{\Xi(\phi)}\Phi=T_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\phi}\Phi
. Applied on a generic
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
it results in,

\displaystyle\Xi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}

\displaystyle=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\right\}^{v}_{|\Xi(\phi)},

\displaystyle=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\star}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}\right\}^{v}_{|\phi}\right).

(26)

The proof is independent from the equivariance of
\Xi\sim\boldsymbol{\psi}
, thus holds the same for
\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\sim\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
.
This relation can be used to obtain the formula for repeated pushforwards: e.g. to obtain the result for
(\Xi^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\circ}\Xi)_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}
, per (
3
), one only needs to substitute
\boldsymbol{\psi}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\circ}R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big)
. In case
\Xi,\Xi^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
, one substitutes
\boldsymbol{\psi}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}
.
This is key to the geometric definition of general vertical transformations and gauge transformations on field space.

2.2.2
Differential forms and their derivations

As a manifold,
\Phi
has a space of forms
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
, together with the graded Lie algebra of its derivations

\Der_{\bullet}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\big)=\bigoplus_{k}\Der_{k}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\big)
whose graded brackets is
[D_{k},D_{l}]=D_{k}\circ D_{l}-(-)^{kl}D_{l}\circ D_{k}
, with
D_{i}\in\Der_{i}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\big)
.

The de Rham complex of
\Phi
is
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi);\boldsymbol{d}\big)
with
\boldsymbol{d}\in\Der_{1}
the de Rham (exterior) derivative, which is nilpotent –
\boldsymbol{d}^{2}=0=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}[\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d}]
– and defined via the Koszul formula. Given the exterior product
\wedge
defined as usual on scalar-valued forms, we have that

\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\mathbb{K}),\wedge,\boldsymbol{d}\big)
is a differential graded algebra.
9
9

9

The exterior product can also be defined on the space
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\sf A)
of variational differential forms with values in an algebra
(\sf A,\cdot)
, using the product in
\sf A
instead of the product in the field
\mathbb{K}
. So
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\sf A),\wedge,\boldsymbol{d}\big)
is a again a differential graded algebra. On the other hand, an exterior product cannot be defined on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V})
where
\boldsymbol{V}
is merely a vector space.

One may define the subset of vector-field valued differential forms
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,T\Phi)=\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\otimes T\Phi
. Then, the subalgebra of
“
algebraic
” derivations is defined as
D_{|\Omega^{0}(\Phi)}=0
, they have the form
\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}\in\Der_{k-1}
for
\boldsymbol{K}\in\Omega^{k}(\Phi,T\Phi)
, with
\iota
the inner product.
For
\omega\otimes\mathfrak{X}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,T\Phi)
we have :
\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\omega\otimes\mathfrak{X}):=\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}\omega\otimes\mathfrak{X}=\boldsymbol{\omega}\circ\boldsymbol{K}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
.
On
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,T\Phi)
, the
Nijenhuis-Richardson bracket
(or
algebraic
bracket) is defined by:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{K},\boldsymbol{L}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}:=\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}\boldsymbol{L}-(-)^{(k-1)(l-1)}\,\iota_{\boldsymbol{L}}\boldsymbol{K}.

(27)

It generalises the inner contraction of a form on a vector field and makes the map,

\displaystyle\iota:\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,T\Phi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Der_{\bullet}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\big)

(28)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}

\displaystyle\mapsto\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}

a graded Lie algebra morphism;

\displaystyle[\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}},\iota_{\boldsymbol{L}}]=\iota_{[\boldsymbol{K},\boldsymbol{L}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}}.

(29)

The
Nijenhuis-Lie derivative
is the map,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}:=[\iota,\boldsymbol{d}]:\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,T\Phi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Der_{\bullet}\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\big)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}

\displaystyle\mapsto\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}}:=\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}\boldsymbol{d}-(-)^{k-1}\boldsymbol{d}\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}

We have
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}}\in\Der_{k}
for
\boldsymbol{K}\in\Omega^{k}(\Phi,T\Phi)
. It generalises the Lie derivative along vector fields,
\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{X}}\in\Der_{0}
.
It is such that
[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}},\boldsymbol{d}]=0
, and it is a morphism of graded Lie algebras:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{J}}]=\boldsymbol{L}_{[\boldsymbol{K},\boldsymbol{J}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}},

(30)

where
[\boldsymbol{K},\boldsymbol{J}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}
is the
Frölicher-Nijenhuis bracket
. Explicitely, for
\boldsymbol{K}=K\otimes\mathfrak{X}\in\Omega^{k}(\Phi,T\Phi)
and
\boldsymbol{J}=J\otimes\mathfrak{Y}\in\Omega^{l}(\Phi,T\Phi)
, it is:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{K},\boldsymbol{J}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}=K\wedge J\otimes[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]+K\wedge\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{X}}J\otimes\mathfrak{Y}-\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{Y}}K\wedge J\otimes\mathfrak{X}+(-)^{k}\big(dK\wedge\iota_{\mathfrak{X}}J\otimes\mathfrak{Y}+\iota_{\mathfrak{Y}}K\wedge dJ\otimes\mathfrak{X}\big).

(31)

We further have the relations:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}},\iota_{\boldsymbol{J}}]

\displaystyle=\iota_{[\boldsymbol{K},\boldsymbol{J}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}}-(-)^{k(l-1)}\boldsymbol{L}_{(\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}\boldsymbol{J})},

(32)

\displaystyle[\iota_{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}}]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{L}_{(\iota_{\boldsymbol{K}}\boldsymbol{J})}+(-)^{k}\,\iota_{[\boldsymbol{J},\boldsymbol{K}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}}.

We refer to
[
Kolar-Michor-Slovak
]
(Chap II, section 8) for a systematic presentation of these notions in the finite dimensional case, and for proofs of the above relations.

The FN bracket (
31
) reproduces as a special case the bracket (
2.2.1
):
Indeed, specialising the above formulae in degree
0
, for
\boldsymbol{f}=f\otimes\mathfrak{X}
and
\boldsymbol{g}=g\otimes\mathfrak{Y}\in\Omega^{0}(\Phi,T\Phi)
we have:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{dg}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{\boldsymbol{f}}\boldsymbol{dg}-(-)^{0}\cancel{\iota_{\boldsymbol{dg}}\boldsymbol{f}}=\big(f\wedge\iota_{\mathfrak{X}}\boldsymbol{d}g\big)\otimes\mathfrak{Y}=\big(f\wedge\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{X}}g\big)\otimes\mathfrak{Y},

(33)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{df},\boldsymbol{g}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}

\displaystyle=\cancel{\iota_{\boldsymbol{df}}\boldsymbol{g}}-(-)^{0}\iota_{\boldsymbol{g}}\boldsymbol{df}=-[\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{df}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}=-\big(g\wedge\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{Y}}f\big)\otimes\mathfrak{X}.

So that,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{g}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}

\displaystyle=f\wedge g\otimes[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]+f\wedge\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{X}}g\otimes\mathfrak{Y}-\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{Y}}f\wedge g\otimes\mathfrak{X},

\displaystyle=f\wedge g\otimes[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]+[\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{dg}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}-[\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{df}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}},

(34)

and

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{f}},\iota_{\boldsymbol{g}}]

\displaystyle=\iota_{[\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{g}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}}.

(35)

Now, the map
|^{v}:C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\rightarrow\Gamma(V\Phi)
,
\boldsymbol{X}\mapsto\boldsymbol{X}^{v}
, allows to think of
\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\in\mathfrak{diff}_{v}(\Phi)
as a (vertical) vector-valued 0-form on
\Phi
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\in\Omega^{0}(\Phi,V\Phi)\subset\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,T\Phi)
.
Therefore, the Nihenhuis-Richardson and Frölicher-Nijenhuis brackets naturally apply: in particular, specialising further eq.(
33
) we have, for
\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\in\Omega^{0}(\Phi,V\Phi)
,

\begin{split}[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}&=\{\iota_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}=\{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}(\boldsymbol{Y})\}^{v},\\
[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}&=-[\boldsymbol{Y}^{v},\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}=-\{\iota_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{X}\}^{v}=-\{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}(\boldsymbol{X})\}^{v},\end{split}

(36)

so that the FN bracket for 0-forms (
2.2.2
) specialises to:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}

\displaystyle=\big([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}}\big)^{v}+[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}-[\boldsymbol{Y}^{v},\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{X}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}},

\displaystyle=\big(-[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}}+\boldsymbol{X}^{v}(\boldsymbol{Y})-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}(\boldsymbol{X})\,\big)^{v}=\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}.

(37)

Then of course we have the following special cases of identities eq.(
29
), (
32
), and (
30
) among derivations in
\Der^{\bullet}
:

\displaystyle[\iota_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dY}\}^{v}}]

\displaystyle=\iota_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{NR}}}}=\iota_{\{\iota_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}},

(38)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\iota_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]

\displaystyle=\iota_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}},

(39)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{L}_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}}=\boldsymbol{L}_{\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}}.

(40)

As one should expect, (
40
) reproduces (
25
).
These identities were derived heuristically in the appendices of various works, sometimes at quite a computational cost.
For example, eq.(
40
)/(
25
) reproduces e.g. eq.(3.1) in
[
Freidel-et-al2021
]
, eq.(2.13) in
[
Freidel-et-al2021bis
]
. We show here that they flow naturally from well-established general geometric structures.
10
10

10

See indeed appendix A of
[
Chandrasekaran-et-al2022
]
, which hints at a similar take.

Remarkable forms

The structure group
\Diff(M)
of
\Phi
acts on a form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
by pullback,
R^{\star}_{\psi}\alpha
, defining its
equivariance
.
The action by pullback of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, defines
general vertical transformations
,
while the action by pullback of
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
similarly defines
gauge transformations
.

Let us express such a generic form at
\phi\in\Phi
as,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi};\phi\big),

(41)

where
\boldsymbol{d}\phi\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi)
is the basis 1-form on
\Phi
(the infinite dimensional analogue of
dx^{\,\mu}
on a manifold
M
), and
\alpha(\ ;\ )
is the functional expression of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, alternating multilinear in the first arguments, and whose dependence in the second argument
\phi
is a priori arbitrary – but often in physics it will be polynomial. Then, its equivariance and general vertical transformation are:

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi^{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi^{\psi}};\,R_{\psi}\phi\big)=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi^{\psi}};\,\phi^{\psi}\big),\quad\text{for }\ \psi\in\Diff(M),

(42)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{|\phi}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)}

\displaystyle=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\Xi(\phi)};\ \Xi(\phi)\big)=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}};\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big),\quad\text{for }\ \Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\sim\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big).

(43)

The infinitesimal equivariance and general vertical transformations are given by the (Nijenhuis-) Lie derivative along the elements of
\Gamma(V\Phi)
generated respectively by
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big|_{\tau=0}\quad\text{ with }\ X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M),\qquad\quad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\Xi_{\tau}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big|_{\tau=0}\quad\text{ with }\ \boldsymbol{X}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big),

(44)

Concrete results may be computed explicitly. But for some types of forms, there are shortcut for structural reasons. In that regard, and as part of the elementary bundle geometry of field space, we may describe forms of special interest.

First,
equivariant
forms are those whose equivariance is well-behaved in some sense.
Standard
equivariant forms are valued in representations
(\rho,\boldsymbol{V})
of the structure group
\Diff(M)
and s.t.:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{eq}}^{\bullet}(\Phi,\rho)\vcentcolon=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V})\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi^{\psi}}=\rho(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi}\,\right\}.

(45)

The infinitesimal version of the equivariance property is
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=-\rho_{*}(X)\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

The latter is a subspace of
twisted
equivariant forms
[
Francois2019_II
]
: their equivariance is controlled by a 1-cocycle for the action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Phi
, i.e. a map:

\displaystyle C:\Phi\times\Diff(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow G,\quad\text{$G$ some Lie group (possibly infinite dimensional).}

(46)

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto C(\phi;\psi)\qquad\text{s.t.}\quad C(\phi;\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)=C(\phi;\psi^{\prime})\cdot C(\phi^{\psi^{\prime}};\psi).

Manifestly,
\phi
-independent 1-cocycles are just group morphisms, thus typical 1-cocycles generalise representations.
From the 1-cocycle property (
46
) follows that
C(\phi;\text{id}_{M})=\text{id}_{G}=C(\phi^{\psi};\text{id}_{M})
, thus that
C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\!=C(\phi^{\psi};\psi^{-1})
.

If
\boldsymbol{V}
is
a
G
-space, twisted equivariant forms are defined as,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi,C)\vcentcolon=\left\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V})\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi^{\psi}}=C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\psi}\right\}.

(47)

The 1-cocycle relation (
46
) ensures compatibility with the right action:
R_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\star}R^{\star}_{\psi}=R^{\star}_{\psi^{\prime}\circ\,\psi}
. The infinitesimal equivariance is
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=-a(X;\phi)\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, where
a(X,\phi):=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,C(\phi,\psi_{\tau})|_{\tau=0}
is a 1-cocycle for the action of
{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
on
\Phi
:

\displaystyle a:\Phi\times{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{g},\quad\text{$\mathfrak{g}$ the Lie algebra of $G$.}

(48)

\displaystyle(\phi,X)

\displaystyle\mapsto a(X;\phi)\qquad\text{s.t.}\quad X^{v}\!\cdot a(Y;\phi)-Y^{v}\!\cdot a(X;\phi)+\big[a(X;\phi)\,,\ a(Y;\phi)\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}=a([X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}};\phi).

The infinitesimal 1-cocycle relation (
48
) ensures compatibility with the right action:
[\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}]=\boldsymbol{L}_{[X^{v},Y^{v}]}=\boldsymbol{L}_{([X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}})^{v}}
. The reader familiar with gauge anomalies may discern that it is a non-Abelian generalisation of the Wess-Zumino consistency condition for a
\Diff(M)
(Einstein) anomaly
a(X;\phi)
– reproduced for
G
Abelian.

The subspace of
invariant
forms are those whose equivariance is trivial:
\Omega_{\text{inv}}^{\bullet}(\Phi)=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\right\}
. Infinitesimally we have
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0
.

The space of of
horizontal
forms is
\Omega_{\text{hor}}^{\bullet}(\Phi)=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\,|\,\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0\right\}
. Now, a form which is both equivariant and horizontal is said
tensorial
. We have thus standard tensorial forms

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{\bullet}(\Phi,\rho)\vcentcolon=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V})\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\rho(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\text{ \& }\ \iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0\,\right\}.

(49)

And similarly, we have the generalisation: the space of
twisted
tensorial forms,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{\bullet}(\Phi,C)\vcentcolon=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V})\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\text{ \& }\ \iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0\,\right\}.

(50)

In either case, we have obviously
\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{0}(\Phi)=\Omega_{\text{eq}}^{0}(\Phi)
.

One observes that any field theory, given by a Lagrangian
L:\Phi\rightarrow\Omega^{n}(M)
,
\phi\mapsto L(\phi)
, with
n=
dim
M
, or an action
S:\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\phi\mapsto S(\phi)
, is actually a twisted tensorial 0-form.
Indeed, let us define
Z\in\Omega^{0}(\Phi,\mathbb{C}^{*})
, i.e.
Z:\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
, by
\phi\mapsto Z(\phi)\vcentcolon=\exp{iS(\phi)}
. The action of
\Diff(M)
is
R^{\star}_{\psi}L=\psi^{*}L
, one may then define the object
C:\Phi\times\Diff(M)\rightarrow U(1)
, by
C(\phi;\psi)\vcentcolon=\exp{-i\int c(\phi;\psi)}
, where
c(\ \,;\psi)\vcentcolon=R^{\star}_{\psi}L-L
.
This implies that
R^{\star}_{\psi}Z=C(\ \,;\psi)^{-1}Z
.
Furthermore, it is easily checked that
c(\phi;\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)=c(\phi;\psi^{\prime})+c(\phi^{\psi^{\prime}};\psi)
, i.e.
C(\phi;\psi)
satisfies (
46
), meaning that it is a
U(1)
-valued
\Diff(M)
-1-cocycle.
Therefore
Z\in\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{0}(\Phi,C)
.

The infinitesimal equivariance is thus
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}Z=-a(X;\phi)\,Z
, with

-a(X;\ \,)=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}C(\ \,;\psi_{\tau})^{-1}\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}i\int c(\ \,;\psi_{\tau})\big|_{\tau=0}
=i\int\tfrac{d}{d\tau}R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}L-L\,\big|_{\tau=0}=i\int\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\psi_{\tau}^{*}L\,\big|_{\tau=0}=i\int\mathfrak{L}_{X}L=\vcentcolon i\int\alpha(X;\ \,)
. In other words,
a(X;\phi)
is the classical
\Diff(M)
-anomaly. In case
Z
is instead the path integral of
S
, the quantity
a(X;\phi)
is the quantum
\Diff(M)
-anomaly, or Einstein anomaly
[
Bertlmann
;
Bonora2023
]
. In both cases, since Lie
U(1)=i\mathbb{R}
is abelian, (
48
)
is
X^{v}\!\cdot a(Y;\phi)-Y^{v}\!\cdot a(X;\phi)=a([X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}$}}};\phi)
, i.e. the Wess-Zumino consistency condition for the anomaly.

Remark that this view of the non-trivial equivariance of
Z=\exp{iS}
relies on a convention for the action of
\Diff(M)
on integrals (over
M
) that is not the one we use (and argue for) in this paper.
11
11

11

This is not the case for the path integral
Z(\phi)=\int\boldsymbol{d}\phi\exp{\tfrac{i}{\hbar}S(\phi)}
defining quantum theories. Even with our convention, giving an invariant action
S
, the twisted equivariance of
Z
comes from the non-trivial transformation of the measure
\boldsymbol{d}\phi
: the 1-cocycle
C(\phi;\psi)\vcentcolon=\exp{-i\int c(\phi;\psi)}
is then the integrated quantum
\Diff(M)
-anomaly. See e.g. chapter 12 of
[
Bertlmann
]
.

We elaborate on this issue in section
2.5
, in connection to a wider discussion of associated bundles (whose space of sections are the 0-degree of the spaces of tensorial forms (
49
)-(
50
)) and of the fundamental representation of
\Diff(M)
.

Let us highlight the fact that the de Rham derivative
\boldsymbol{d}
does not preserve the space of tensorial forms (one loses the property of horizontality).
12
12

12

This is easy to check: starting on a 1-form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, using the Koszul formula for
\boldsymbol{d}
we have that for any
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
:
\boldsymbol{d\alpha}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})=(\boldsymbol{L}_{\mathfrak{X}}\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\mathfrak{Y})-\mathfrak{Y}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\mathfrak{X})
. So, for
\mathfrak{X}=X^{v}
,
\boldsymbol{d\alpha}(X^{v},\mathfrak{Y})=(\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\mathfrak{Y})-\mathfrak{Y}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X^{v})
. If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is horizontal,
\boldsymbol{d\alpha}(X^{v},\mathfrak{Y})=(\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha})(\mathfrak{Y})
and is non-zero unless
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is also invariant. See next.

This is one reason one needs to introduce an adequate notion of
connection
on
\Phi
so as to define a
covariant derivative
on the space of tensorial forms. For standard tensorial forms, one needs an Ehresmann connection 1-form, while for twisted tensorial forms one needs a generalisation called
twisted
connection
[
Francois2019_II
]
. See section
2.4
below.

Finally, forms that are both invariant and horizontal are called
basic
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{basic}}^{\bullet}(\Phi)\vcentcolon=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\text{ \& }\ \iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0\,\right\}.

(51)

This space
is
preserved by
\boldsymbol{d}
,
13
13

13

See previous footnoote. In other words,
\boldsymbol{d}
is a covariant derivative for basic forms!
which means that

\big(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi),\boldsymbol{d}\big)
is a subcomplex of the de Rham complex of
\Phi
: the
basic subcomplex
.
Alternatively, basic forms can be defined as Im
(\pi^{\star})
(hence the name), that is:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\text{basic}}^{\bullet}(\Phi)\vcentcolon=\left\{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\,|\,\exists\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})\text{ s.t. }\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\pi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\beta}\,\right\}.

(52)

Since
[\boldsymbol{d},\pi^{\star}]=0
, the cohomology of the basic subcomplex is isomorphic to the cohomology
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}),\boldsymbol{d}\big)
of the moduli space: The cohomology of
\big(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi),\boldsymbol{d}\big)
is known as the
equivariant cohomology
of
\Phi
.

Remark that the “form” analogue of
\Gamma_{\text{\!\tiny{inv}}}(T\Phi)
– projecting to well defined vector fields
\Gamma(TM)
– is not
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
,
but
\Omega_{\text{basic}}^{\bullet}(\Phi)
: Only the latter, per the second definition, projects to well-defined forms in
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
.
It will be a main endeavor of this paper to show how, given some form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
, to formally built its basic counterpart
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}\in\Omega_{\text{basic}}^{\bullet}(\Phi)
. This will be achieved via the dressing field method, section
3
.

2.3
General vertical transformations, and gauge transformations

As already stated above, the general vertical transformation a form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
is its pullback by
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
:
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.
The notation on the left-hand side is operationally defined by the right-hand side, and justified by the fact that the vertical transformation is expressible in term of the generating element
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
associated to
\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
.
Performing two vertical transformations, using (
3
), one has

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big)^{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}}\vcentcolon={\Xi^{\prime}}^{\star}\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\big(\Xi\boldsymbol{\circ}\Xi^{\prime}\big)^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}\circ\,(\boldsymbol{\psi}\boldsymbol{\circ}R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}})}.

(53)

In the case of gauge transformations defined by the action of
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
, since the defining equivariance of gauge group elements is
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\boldsymbol{\psi}\boldsymbol{\circ}R_{\psi}=\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\psi
, the above expression simplifies to a more familiar form

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big)^{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\circ\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}}.

(54)

Naturally, infinitesimal general vertical transformations are given by the action of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
via the Nijenhuis-Lie derivative:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\left\{\begin{matrix}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\Xi_{\tau}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\big|_{\tau=0}\\[8.53581pt]
\hskip-5.69046pt[\iota_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{d}]\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\end{matrix}\right.\qquad\text{so}\qquad[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{L}_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{\tiny{FN}}}}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{L}_{\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

(55)

The second equation, using (
25
)/(
40
), is the infinitesimal reflection of (
53
).
For infinitesimal gauge transformations, given by the action of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
, this simplifies to

[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{L}_{(-[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}})^{v}}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{L}_{([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}})^{v}}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

To be more explicit, it is possible to take a further step: For any
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\ldots\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
, given (
2.2.1
) and the duality between pullback and pushforward, one has that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi},\ldots)=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi},\ldots)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)}(\Xi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi},\ldots)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}}\left(R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\star}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}\right\}^{v}_{|\phi}\right),\ldots\right),

(56)

\displaystyle=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}^{\star}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}\right\}^{v}_{|\phi},\ldots\right).

Cleary therefore, the vertical transformation of a form is controlled by its equivariance and verticality properties.
In particular, from (
56
) follows that the vertical transformation of a
tensorial
form is simply given by its equivariance:

\begin{split}&\text{For }\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{\bullet}(\Phi,\rho),\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}.\\
&\text{For }\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{\bullet}(\Phi,C),\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}.\end{split}

(57)

In the second line we introduced the shorter notation
[C(\boldsymbol{\psi})](\phi)\vcentcolon=C\big(\phi;\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\big)
, so the map
C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
has thus a double dependency on the point
\phi\in\Phi
.
Due to their horizontality, their vertical transformation is homogeneous, tensorial so to speak, hence their name.
It should be remarked that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\notin\Omega_{\text{tens}}^{\bullet}(\Phi,\rho)
unless
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)\sim\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
– see
[
Francois2023-b
]
– making
gauge transformations
special indeed.

It is standard, and easy to show, that the infinitesimal versions of (
56
) is, by definition (
55
), simply,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\big|_{\tau=0}+\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

(58)

where of course
\boldsymbol{X}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\tau}\big|_{\tau=0}\!
, and
\boldsymbol{\psi}=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\in\Diff(M)
in the equivariance term is
\phi
-constant.
Remark that
\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}
can be considered an element of
\Omega^{1}(\Phi,V\Phi)
, so
\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}}
is an algebraic derivation (of degree 0) of the type discussed in section
2.2.2
.
Again, it is clear that a vertical transformation depends on both the equivariance and the verticality properties of a form.
For
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}(\phi)
, depending if it is standard or twisted equivariant, (
58
) specialises to:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\left\{\begin{matrix}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\\[8.53581pt]
-a(\boldsymbol{X})\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\end{matrix}\right.

(59)

where we introduce the notation
[a(\boldsymbol{X})](\phi)\vcentcolon=a\big(\boldsymbol{X}(\phi);\phi\big)
for the linearised 1-cocycle.
And in particular for
\rho(X)^{-1}=\psi^{*}
the pullback representation, i.e. for
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
- (or tensor-) valued forms
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, (
58
) gives naturally:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

(60)

We remark that the above formula features in an indirect way in (and clarify the meaning of) the so-called “anomaly operator” appearing in recent covariant phase space literature:

\Delta_{\boldsymbol{X}}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}-\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{dX}\}^{v}}
in our notations
– compare e.g. to
[
Hopfmuller-Freidel2018
]
eq.(2.39),
[
Chandrasekaran_Speranza2021
]
eq.(2.1),
[
Freidel-et-al2021
]
eq.(2.13),
[
Freidel-et-al2021bis
]
eq.(2.3), or
[
Speziale-et-al2023
]
eq.(2.2).
Of course, this operator can be non-zero on
\Phi
only in theories that are
fundamentally
“non-general-relativisitic” in admitting background non-dynamical structures or fields
“breaking”

\Diff(M)
-covariance (see section
3
for further elaboration on this comment).

The infinitesimal versions of (
57
), for tensorial forms, are then obviously:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{\alpha},\quad\text{or }\quad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=-a(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{\alpha},

(61)

From applying (
25
)/(
40
), the commutativity property of the Nijenhuis-Lie derivative, on a twisted tensorial form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
:

[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{L}_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{{\tiny FN}}}}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}

=\boldsymbol{L}_{\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, we get the following relation for the infinitesimal 1-cocycle,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(a(\boldsymbol{Y};\phi)\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(a(\boldsymbol{X};\phi)\big)-a(\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\};\phi)+[a(\boldsymbol{X};\phi),a(\boldsymbol{Y};\phi)]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}=0,

(62)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(a(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{Y}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\phi)\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(a(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{X}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\phi)\big)-a([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}};\phi)+[a(\boldsymbol{X};\phi),a(\boldsymbol{Y};\phi)]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}=0.

One obtains the second line from the first using the FN bracket (
2.2.2
)/(
2.2.1
): The notation
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{Y}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{X}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is meant to indicate that the element
\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{X}
are considered
\phi
-independent in the formula, so that
\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}
pass through it. Therefore, (
62
) simply reproduces the defining infinitesimal 1-cocycle property (
48
) even for
\phi
-dependent
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
-parameters.

As examples, we may consider the case of elements of the gauge group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
and its Lie algebra
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
. These are 0-forms, so trivially horizontal, and their equivariance are specified by definition: they are thus tensorial 0-forms. For
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
whose equivariance is given by
\psi
-conjugation, see (
4
), and for
\boldsymbol{Y}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
whose equivariance if given by
\psi
-relatedness, see (
15
), we have thus:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\eta}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi},\qquad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{Y}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})_{*}\boldsymbol{Y}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}

(63)

The infinitesimal gauge transformation of
\boldsymbol{Y}
is thus:
