\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{Y}=\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}=[\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{X}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}
– in coherence with its infinitesimal equivariance given by (
16
).

And as a special case of the above, or given their definition, basic forms are strictly gauge invariant:

\displaystyle\text{For }\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega_{\text{basic}}^{\bullet}(\Phi):\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha},\quad\text{so}\quad L_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0.

(64)

That is another way to detect a variational form who would induce (or come from) a corresponding form on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}
, where presumably physical degree of freedom (d.o.f.) live.

An example worth looking at closely is that of the basis 1-form
\boldsymbol{d}\phi\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi)
,
given that its vertical transformation
\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\phi
intervene in the general formulae (
43
) for the vertical transformation of a generic form.
To compute it geometrically via (
56
) we must know the equivariance and verticality properties of
\boldsymbol{d}\phi
. These are given by definition:

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{d}\phi\vcentcolon=\psi^{*}\boldsymbol{d}\phi,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{\iota}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi.

(65)

That is, the equivariance is controlled by the pullback representation, while the verticality just reproduces the infinitesimal (field-independent) diffeo/gauge transformation of the field
\phi
.
It is therefore almost immediate that its vertical transformation is,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\phi=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi+\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}]}\phi\big).

(66)

It is maybe pedagogically useful to see it unfold in more detail:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})=\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\Xi(\phi)}(\Xi_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}}\left(R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\star}\big(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}\right\}^{v}_{|\phi}\big)\right),

\displaystyle=R_{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)}}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}\right\}^{v}_{|\phi}\right),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{*}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}+\left\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}\right\}^{v}_{|\phi}\right),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{*}\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}]}\phi,

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi}+\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{|\phi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{-1}]}\phi\big)\right)(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}),

This reproduces a result now standard in the literature on the covariant phase space of gravity: See e.g. eq.(3.5) and (3.6) in
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]
where the relation is proven in Appendix B via more heuristic algebraic computations. Notice the gain in efficiency stemming from the geometric approach.
By (
60
), the infinitesimal version is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi=\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{X}}\phi.

(67)

The same could be found via
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi=\boldsymbol{d}(\iota_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi)=\boldsymbol{d}\big(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\phi\big)
.

2.4
Connections on field space

As we’ve observed above, the exterior derivative
\boldsymbol{d}
does not preserve
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi)
of standard/twisted tensorial forms. To build a first order linear differential operator that does, the covariant derivative, one needs to endow
\Phi
with an adequate notion of connection 1-form.

2.4.1
Ehresmann connections

A Ehresmann connection 1-form
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
is defined by:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{|\phi}\big(X^{v}_{|\phi}\big)

\displaystyle=X,\quad\text{for }X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M),

(68)

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{|\phi^{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\psi^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{|\phi}\circ\,\psi.

Infinitesimally, the equivariance of the connection under
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
is,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\omega}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\omega}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,{\psi_{\tau}^{-1}}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}\circ\,\psi_{\tau}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=[X,\boldsymbol{\omega}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}}=[\boldsymbol{\omega},X]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}.

(69)

The space of connection
\mathcal{C}
is an affine space modelled on the vector space
\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
: Indeed, it is clear that for
\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that
\boldsymbol{\beta}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
. Or, given
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
, we have that
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\omega}+\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathcal{C}
. This means that in general one cannot add connections.
14
14

14

Only affine combinations like
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\tau}\vcentcolon=\tau\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}+(1-\tau)\boldsymbol{\omega}=\boldsymbol{\omega}+\tau\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
\tau\in[0,1],
are possible. Then the midpoint
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\tau=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\omega}+\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime})
is an averaged sum of two connections.

A connection allows to define the horizontal subbundle
H\Phi\vcentcolon=\ker\boldsymbol{\omega}
complementary to the vertical subbundle,
T\Phi=V\Phi\oplus H\Phi
. The horizontal projection is thus the map
|^{h}:T\Phi\rightarrow H\Phi
,
\mathfrak{X}\mapsto\mathfrak{X}^{h}\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{X}-[\boldsymbol{\omega}(\mathfrak{X})]^{v}
, as clearly
\boldsymbol{\omega}(\mathfrak{X}^{h})=0
.

The covariant derivative is thus defined as
\boldsymbol{D}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\circ}|^{h}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,\rho)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\rho)
. On tensorial forms is has the familiar expression,
\boldsymbol{D}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\rho)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\rho)
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mapsto\boldsymbol{D\alpha}=\boldsymbol{d\alpha}+\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Which is the first order linear operator we were looking for.

The curvature 2-form is defined as
\boldsymbol{\Omega}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d\omega}\boldsymbol{\circ}|^{h}
, from which follows that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}\in\Omega^{2}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
. As is well known, it is also given by Cartan structure equation,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{d\omega}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{\omega}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}.

(70)

From this it is easy to see that it satisfies Bianchi identity,
\boldsymbol{D\Omega}=\boldsymbol{d\Omega}+[\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{\Omega}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}=0
. On tensorial forms we have
\boldsymbol{D}\circ\boldsymbol{D}=\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\Omega})
. We may remark that the FN bracket (
2.2.2
)/(
2.2.1
) plays a key role in the last step of proving (
70
), which requires to show that both sides of the equality vanish on
\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
with
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
: It is the case of the left-hand side by definition – since
(\boldsymbol{X}^{v})^{h}\equiv 0
– while for the right-hand side one has,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d\omega}\big(\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big)+[\boldsymbol{\omega}(\boldsymbol{X}^{v}),\boldsymbol{\omega}(\boldsymbol{Y}^{v})]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(\omega(\boldsymbol{Y}^{v})\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(\omega(\boldsymbol{X}^{v})\big)-\boldsymbol{\omega}\big([\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]\big)+[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(\boldsymbol{Y}\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(\boldsymbol{X}\big)-\boldsymbol{\omega}\big(\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}\big)+[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}+\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(\boldsymbol{Y}\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(\boldsymbol{X}\big)-\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}\equiv 0.

The Koszul formula for
\boldsymbol{d}
was used in the first line, and (
2.2.1
) in the last to conclude.

Given the defining equivariance and verticality properties (
68
) of a connection, it is easy, using (
2.2.1
)/(
56
), to see that its general vertical transformation under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\omega}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d\psi}.

(71)

Naturally, the formula is the same for its gauge transformation under
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
: The difference arises upon repeated transformations of each type, as we stressed in section
2.3
, see (
53
)–(
54
).
Here again, it should be remarked that
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\omega}\notin\mathcal{C}
unless
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)\sim\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
– see
[
Francois2023-b
]
– highlithing again the special status of
gauge transformations
.
Infinitesimally, by (
55
), transformations of a connection under
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
are given by the Nijenhuis-Lie derivative,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\omega}=\boldsymbol{dX}+[\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{X}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}.

(72)

Infinitesimal gauge transformations, under
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
, are given by the same relation, but can be written
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\omega}=\boldsymbol{DX}
as
\boldsymbol{X}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
is a tensorial 0-form.
In the same way, finite and infinitesimal general vertical transformations of the curvature are given, as special cases of (
57
) and (
61
), by:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi},\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}=[\boldsymbol{\Omega},\boldsymbol{X}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}.

(73)

The same hold for its gauge transformations, with the same caveat expressed above.

The above, eq. (
71
), allows to write a little lemma that we will occasionally use: For
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{D\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,\rho)
,
we have on the one hand
\boldsymbol{d}\,\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{d}\big(\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)
. On the other hand, by
\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{D\alpha}=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{D\alpha}
, we have that

\displaystyle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}

\displaystyle=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{D\alpha}-\Xi^{\star}\big(\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}+\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\,\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi})\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},

\displaystyle=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{d\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right).

By naturality of the pullback
[\Xi^{\star},\boldsymbol{d}]=0
, we obtain the identity:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\big(\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{d\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right)

(74)

In particular, for the pullback representation,
\rho(\psi)^{-1}\!=\psi^{*}
and
-\rho_{*}(X)=\mathfrak{L}_{X}
, this is:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{d\alpha}+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right).

(75)

The latter special case of the lemma appears in the covariant phase space literature, e.g. in
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]
and
[
Speranza2018
]
.
Despite the superficial similarity, it ought not to be confused with with (
66
) as the two results are distinct geometric statements.

Horizontalisation of forms.

The definition of the curvature showcases the operation dual to the horizontal projection for vector fields:
the horizontalisation of forms,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}|}^{h}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{hor}}(\Phi),\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\circ}|^{h}.

(76)

Of course,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}|}^{h}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi)
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}|}^{h}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi)
. So, a connection can be used to extract the
basic
version of some forms, invariant ones.

In recent years, this has been used in the covariant phase space literature to offer a possible solution of the boundary problem arising in the study of the (pre)symplectic structure of a gauge field theory over a bounded region: The technical core of the problem being that the invariant presymplectic potential and 2-forms lacks horizontality due to boundary terms contributions to their verticality properties, thus fail to be invariant under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
or
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
. An issue we will spell out in more detail in section
4
.
The proposal, developed by Gomes and Riello, was to use a connection to horizontalize the presymplectic potential – see
[
Gomes-Riello2017
;
Gomes-Riello2018
;
Gomes-et-al2018
;
Gomes-Riello2021
]
, and
[
Gomes2019
;
Gomes2019-bis
;
Gomes2021
]
for a more philosophical discussion of the proposal.

For a 1-form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi)
we have the explicit expression in term of the connection

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\omega}(\ )]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{hor}}(\Phi).

(77)

In particular for the basis 1-form
\boldsymbol{d}\phi\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi)
, with properties (
65
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d}\phi-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\omega}(\ )]^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi=\boldsymbol{d}\phi-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\phi\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi).

(78)

We remark that applying the covariant derivative to it, as a tensorial form, one finds that
\boldsymbol{D}(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h})=-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\phi
.
So, from (
78
) we have that the horizontalisation map (
76
) is also written explicitly as,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi\big)\quad\mapsto\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h};\phi\big).

(79)

Then, (
77
) is also written as,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\alpha\big(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\phi;\phi\big)\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{hor}}(\Phi).

(80)

This relation is key to the horizontalisation of the presymplectic potential
\boldsymbol{\theta}
via a connection, i.e. to the the extraction of a
basic counterpart

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\ \!\!b}
of
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
.
For an invariant 1-form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
, we have
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}\ \in\Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi)
and one shows that the following relation between the covariant derivative and the horizontalised form holds,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}+\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\Omega}]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}+\alpha(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\phi;\phi).

(81)

Therefore for a flat connection
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{D}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}
.
The formula (
81
) generalises the special case applying to the presymplectic potential
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{b}
discussed by Gomes & Riello (in the YM case) in corollary 3.2 and section 3.4 of
[
Gomes-Riello2021
]
, also at the end of section 3.1. in
[
Riello2021
]
.

Non-uniqueness.

One may highlight that since a priori the choice of connection is not unique (nor canonical),
15
15

15

Unless there is a natural choice, as would be e.g. the case if a connection was derived from a natural metric on
\Phi
: This is what happens in the Yang-Mills (YM) case, where
\Phi
is the space of connection
\mathcal{A}
of a
H
-bundle
P
, on which a bundle metric induces the Singer-DeWitt connection. This is the approach followed in
[
Gomes-Riello2017
;
Gomes-Riello2018
;
Gomes-et-al2018
;
Gomes-Riello2021
]
. See section 2.2 of
[
Francois-et-al2021
]
for a discussion.
nor is the horizontalisation map (
76
).
For any
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
, both
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\omega}+\boldsymbol{\beta}
are valid choices to implement (
76
). Given
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
, the corresponding horizontal forms obtained are
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}
.

One may ask how they are related.
The answer is easily found for 1-forms:
Consider the affine path
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\tau}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{\omega}+\tau\boldsymbol{\beta}
, with
\tau\in[0,1]
, in
\mathcal{C}
. Given
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi)
, define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\tau}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\tau}]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\quad\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{hor}}(\Phi).

\displaystyle\text{So, }\quad\textstyle\int_{0}^{1}d\tau\ \tfrac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\tau}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\beta}]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\beta}]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\quad\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{hor}}(\Phi).

(82)

In particular, for the basis 1-form
\boldsymbol{d}\phi\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi)
we get

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\beta}]^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}\phi=\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\phi\quad\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi).

(83)

From this, one can work out the precise relation between
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}\,;\,\phi\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\,;\,\phi\big)
.
In the case
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi\big)\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
, we thus have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\beta}]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\quad\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\alpha\big(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\phi;\phi\big).

(84)

And, since on basic forms
\boldsymbol{D}=\boldsymbol{d}
, we have that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{d}\alpha\big(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\phi;\phi\big)\quad\in\Omega^{2}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi).

(85)

This result generalises the special case
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{b}
(for YM theories) discussed in section 6.7 in
[
Gomes-Riello2021
]
(compare also to eq.(83) of
[
Riello2021
]
), where it is interpreted as a “gluing relation” between the basic presymplectic structures stemming from different choices of connections made by different observers on adjacent regions.

After this digression connecting to recent literature on covariant phase space, let us come back to our initial question and discuss next the connections adapted to twisted tensorial forms.

2.4.2
Twisted connections

We remind that twisted equivariant/tensorial forms
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
have values in a
G
-space
\boldsymbol{V}
,
G
a (possibly infinite dimensional) Lie group,
and their equivariance is given by a 1-cocycle (
46
)
for the action of
{\Diff}(M)
on
\Phi
,

C:\Phi\times\Diff(M)\rightarrow G
,
(\phi,\psi)\mapsto C(\phi;\psi)
, such that

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\qquad\text{with}\quad C(\phi;\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)=C(\phi;\psi^{\prime})\cdot C(\phi^{\psi^{\prime}};\psi).

(86)

Their infinitesimal equivariance is then given by
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=-a(X,\phi)\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, with

a(X,\phi):=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,C(\phi,\psi_{\tau})|_{\tau=0}
a 1-cocycle (
48
) for the action of
{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
on
\Phi
.
As we remarked above, in section
2.2.2
, Lagrangians/actions are twisted 0-forms: A Lagrangian being clearly always
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
-anomalous.

In particular, if the target group of the 1-cocycle is a (subgroup of the) diffeomorphism group,
G=\Diff(M)
, and
\boldsymbol{V}
is a space of tensors of
M
, (
86
) specialises to

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=C(\phi;\psi)^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\qquad\text{with}\quad C(\phi;\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)=C(\phi;\psi^{\prime})\circ C(\phi^{\psi^{\prime}};\psi).

(87)

These, we may observe, is the type of mathematical objects that underly the “relational observables” introduced in
[
Hoehn-et-al2022
]
:
Indeed, eq.(3.183)-(3.185) in section 3.4.4. displays the fact that, up to a left/right action convention, these ‘‘relational observables” are twisted equivariant/tensorial 0-forms.
16
16

16

We have a right action convention, so that e.g. if
\boldsymbol{V}
are purely vectors the equivariance (
87
) is
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=[C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}]_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, to be compared to the equivariance eq.(3.183) in
[
Hoehn-et-al2022
]
. Compare also the cocycle property (
86
) to their eq.(3.185).

Then, eq.(3.190) is an instance of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
transformation (
57
) of twisted tensorial forms.

A
twisted
connection 1-form
\boldsymbol{\varpi}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{g}\big)
is defined by:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi}\big(X^{v}_{|\phi}\big)

\displaystyle=a(X,\phi)\ \in\mathfrak{g},\quad\text{for }X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M),

(88)

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi^{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\text{Ad}\big(C(\phi;\psi)\big)^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi}+C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{d}C(\ \,;\psi)_{|\phi}.

In the special case
G=\Diff(M)
, the equivariance of
\boldsymbol{\varpi}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
is

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi^{\psi}}

\displaystyle=[C(\phi;\psi)\big)^{-1}]_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi}\circ C(\phi;\psi)+[C(\phi;\psi)\big)^{-1}]_{*}\boldsymbol{d}C(\ \,;\psi)_{|\phi},

(89)

to be compared to that (
68
) of an Ehresmann connection.
The infinitesimal equivariance under
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\varpi}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\varpi}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\boldsymbol{d}a(X;\ )+[\boldsymbol{\varpi},a(X;\ )]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}

(90)

Or, in the case
G=\Diff(M)
,
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\varpi}=\boldsymbol{d}a(X;\ )+[\boldsymbol{\varpi},a(X;\ )]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}
.

The space of twisted connection
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
is an affine space modelled on the vector space
\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{g}\big)
: Clearly, for
\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that
\boldsymbol{\beta}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{g}\big)
. Or, given
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{g}\big)
, we have that
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\omega}+\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. This means that in general one cannot add connections, only affine paths are possible.

A
twisted covariant derivative
is defined as
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,C)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,C)
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mapsto\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d\alpha}+\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. This is the first order linear operator adapted to twisted equivariant/tensorial forms.

As a relevant example, consider
Z=\exp{iS}\in\Omega^{0}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,C)
as defined earlier to illustrate (
50
): we claim that
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}Z\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,C)
.
Indeed, in this case
\boldsymbol{\varpi}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,i\mathbb{R}\big)
, so (
88
) gives

\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi}\big(X^{v}_{|\phi}\big)=a(X,\phi)=-i\int\alpha(X;\phi)
– the classical/quantum
\Diff(M)
-anomaly – and

R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi^{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{|\phi}-i\boldsymbol{d}c(\ \,;\psi)_{|\phi}
. From this is easily verified that
R^{\star}_{\psi}\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}Z=C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\!\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}Z
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}Z(X^{v})=0
.
We may remark that, in this case,
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}Z=(i\boldsymbol{d}S+\boldsymbol{\varpi})Z
. Now since
Z
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}Z
are both tensorial, it means that

\displaystyle i\boldsymbol{d}S+\boldsymbol{\varpi}\ \in\Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}\big(\Phi).

(91)

This is a kind of generalisation of the Wess-Zumino trick to build counterterms and “improve” and action by restoring its gauge invariance: the original trick is recovered for flat
\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}
, equivalent to a dressing field, as we show in section
3.1
below.
Conversely, the WZ trick is seen to come from a special case of twisted covariant derivative.

Contrary to an Ehresmann connection, a twisted connection does not to defined an equivariant horizontal distribution on
\Phi
. Yet, its curvature 2-form can be defined, via Cartan structure equation

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d\varpi}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\boldsymbol{\varpi},\boldsymbol{\varpi}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}\ \ \in\Omega^{2}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{g}\big)

(92)

It satisfies Bianchi identity,
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=\boldsymbol{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}+[\boldsymbol{\varpi},\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}]_{\text{{\tiny$LieG$}}}=0
. And it is still true that
\boldsymbol{D}\circ\boldsymbol{D}=\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\Omega})
.
We may observe that the horizontality of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
encode interesting information. For
X^{v},Y^{v}\in\Gamma(V\Phi)
with
X,Y\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}(X^{v},Y^{v})

\displaystyle=X^{v}\big(\omega(Y^{v})\big)-Y^{v}\big(\omega(X^{v})\big)-\boldsymbol{\omega}\big([X^{v},Y^{v}]\big)+[\boldsymbol{\omega}(X^{v}),\boldsymbol{\omega}(Y^{v})]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=X^{v}\big(a(Y;\phi)\big)-Y^{v}\big(a(X;\phi)\big)-a([X,Y]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}};\phi)+[a(X;\phi),a(Y;\phi)]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}},

(93)

which reproduces the infinitesimal 1-cocycle property (
48
) generalizing the Wess-Zumino consistency condition on gauge/
\Diff(M)
anomalies.

Given the defining equivariance and verticality properties (
88
) of a twisted connection, one shows using (
2.2.1
)/(
56
), that its general vertical transformation under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varpi}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\varpi}=\text{Ad}\big(C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\big)\,\boldsymbol{\varpi}+C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{\psi}),

(94)

where we remind the short-hand notation
[C(\boldsymbol{\psi})](\phi)\vcentcolon=C\big(\phi;\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)\big)
. In case
G=\Diff(M)
the above specialises to:
\boldsymbol{\varpi}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\varpi}\circ C(\boldsymbol{\psi})+C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
.
Gauge transformations under
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
are given by the same formula: The difference being noticed upon repeated transformations of each type, as stressed by (
53
)–(
54
) in section
2.3
.
Infinitesimally, transformations of a twisted connection under
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
are given by the Nijenhuis-Lie derivative,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\varpi}=\boldsymbol{d}a(\boldsymbol{X})+[\boldsymbol{\varpi},a(\boldsymbol{X})]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}.

(95)

where we remind that
[a(\boldsymbol{X})](\phi)\vcentcolon=a\big(\boldsymbol{X}(\phi);\phi\big)
.
Infinitesimal gauge transformations, under
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
, are given by the same relation.
The difference being seen upon iteration, as reflected by the commutation property (
25
)/(
40
) of the Nijenhuis-Lie derivative along
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
,
[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]=\boldsymbol{L}_{[\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]_{\text{{\tiny$FN$}}}}=\boldsymbol{L}_{\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\}^{v}}
, which, in the case
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}(M)
, simplifies
[\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}},\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}}]=\boldsymbol{L}_{(-[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{\tiny{$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}})^{v}}=\boldsymbol{L}_{([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\Gamma(TM)$}}})^{v}}
– as we observed in section
2.3
.

Finite and infinitesimal general vertical transformations of the curvature are given by,

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=\text{Ad}\big(C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\big)\,\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}},\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=[\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}},a(\boldsymbol{X})]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}.

(96)

or,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}
and
\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=[\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}},\boldsymbol{X}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}
in the case
G=\Diff(M)
.
These are special cases of (
57
) and (
61
). The same hold for its gauge transformations, with the usual caveat.

For
\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
with
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}(\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(\omega(\boldsymbol{Y}^{v})\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(\omega(\boldsymbol{X}^{v})\big)-\boldsymbol{\omega}\big([\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}]\big)+[\boldsymbol{\omega}(\boldsymbol{X}^{v}),\boldsymbol{\omega}(\boldsymbol{Y}^{v})]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}

(97)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(a(\boldsymbol{Y};\phi)\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(a(\boldsymbol{X};\phi)\big)-a(\{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\};\phi)+[a(\boldsymbol{X};\phi),a(\boldsymbol{Y};\phi)]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}^{v}\big(a(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{Y}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\phi)\big)-\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\big(a(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{X}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\phi)\big)-a([\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}};\phi)+[a(\boldsymbol{X};\phi),a(\boldsymbol{Y};\phi)]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}},

where the FN bracket (
2.2.2
)/(
2.2.1
) is used.
This reproduces (
62
), where the notation of the last line was first used.
As the relation holds for
\boldsymbol{X}^{v},\boldsymbol{Y}^{v}\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{aut}}_{v}(\Phi)
, the horizontality properties (
93
)-(
97
) of the twisted curvature therefore encode the same generalisation of the Wess-Zumino consistency condition.

2.5
Associated bundles, fondamental representation of
\Diff(M)
, and integration on
M

Starting from a principal bundle, one of the most natural construction one can perform is that of its associated bundles. The standard theory specify how a class of them is built from representations of the structure group, while a natural extension of this classic construction involve the use of 1-cocycles for the action of the structure group instead of representations
[
Francois2019_II
]
. Let us consider them in turn in our case, where the principal bundle is
\Phi
with structure group
\Diff(M)
.

Given a representation space
(\rho,\boldsymbol{V})
of
\Diff(M)
, consider the direct product space
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
, with the two natural projections:
\pi_{\Phi}:\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\rightarrow\Phi
and
\pi_{\boldsymbol{V}}:\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{V}
.
One defines a right action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
by:

\displaystyle\big(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times\Diff(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V},

(98)

\displaystyle\big((\phi,v),\psi\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\big)=\big(R_{\psi}\phi,\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\big)=\vcentcolon\bar{R}_{\psi}(\phi,v).

The bundle
\boldsymbol{E}
associated to
\Phi
via the representation
\rho
is then defined as the quotient of the product space
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
by this right action:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}=\Phi\times_{\rho}\boldsymbol{V}\vcentcolon=\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}/\!\sim

(99)

where
(\phi^{\prime},v^{\prime})\sim(\phi,v)
when
\exists\,\psi\in\Diff(M)
s.t.
(\phi^{\prime},v^{\prime})=\bar{R}_{\psi}(\phi,v)
.
We may write
\bar{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{E}}:\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{E}
.
A point in
\boldsymbol{E}
is thus an equivalence class
e=[\phi,v]
. The projection of
\boldsymbol{E}\xrightarrow{\pi_{\boldsymbol{E}}}\mathcal{M}
is defined by
\pi_{\boldsymbol{E}}([\phi,v])\vcentcolon=\pi(\phi)=[\phi]
.
It is a standard result of bundle theory that sections of
\boldsymbol{E}
are in 1:1 correspondence with
V
-valued
\rho
-equivariant functions on
\Phi
:

\displaystyle\Gamma(\boldsymbol{E})\vcentcolon=\big\{\boldsymbol{s}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{E}\big\}\ \ \simeq\ \ \Omega^{0}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,\rho\big)\vcentcolon=\big\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}:\Phi\rightarrow V\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\rho(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\big\}.

(100)

The isomorphism being
\boldsymbol{s}([\phi])=[\phi,\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\phi)]
.
Naturally, equivariant functions are tensorial 0-forms, and like all equivariant forms their vertical transformations (gauge transformations) is given by (
57
)-(
61
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\varphi},\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{\varphi}.

(101)

for
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
and
\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
.
A principal connection as discussed in section
2.4.1
is needed for their covariant differentiation.

The right action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
defines the vertical subbundle
V(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V})\simeq V\Phi\oplus V\boldsymbol{V}\subset T(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V})\simeq T\Phi\oplus T\boldsymbol{V}
. By linearisation of (
98
), it is:

\displaystyle\big(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times\mathfrak{diff}(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow V(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V})\simeq V\Phi\oplus V\boldsymbol{V},

(102)

\displaystyle\big((\phi,v),X\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\ X^{v}_{|(\phi,v)}\vcentcolon=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\bar{R}_{\psi_{\tau}}(\phi,v)\,\big|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\big(\psi_{\tau}^{*}\phi,\,\rho(\psi_{\tau})^{-1}v\big)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\big(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi,v\big)\oplus\big(\phi,-\rho_{*}(X)v\big),

\displaystyle=\left((\phi,v);\big(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi,-\rho_{*}(X)v\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\big(\phi;\mathfrak{L}_{X}\phi\big)\oplus\big(v;-\rho_{*}(X)v\big)=\vcentcolon X^{v}_{|\phi}+X^{v}_{|v}.

Therefore, the tangent bundle of
\boldsymbol{E}
is defined as
T\boldsymbol{E}\vcentcolon=T(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V})/V(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V})

The whole construction carries through if one replaces the representation
\rho
by a 1-cocycle
\ C\!:\Phi\times\Diff(M)\rightarrow G
, as defined by (
46
) in section
2.2.2
.
If
\boldsymbol{V}
is a
G
-space, one may define a right action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
via:

\big(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times\Diff(M)\rightarrow\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
,
\big((\phi,v),\psi\big)\mapsto\bar{R}_{\psi}(\phi,v)=\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\,C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}v\big)
.
The
twisted
bundle
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
associated to
\Phi
via the 1-cocycle
C
is then defined as:

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}=\Phi\times_{C}\boldsymbol{V}\vcentcolon=\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}/\!\sim\,
,
with
\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\,C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}v\big)\sim(\phi,v)
.
As above we have the isomorphism,

\displaystyle\Gamma\big(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\big)\vcentcolon=\big\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\big\}\ \ \simeq\ \ \Omega^{0}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,C\big)\vcentcolon=\big\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}:\Phi\rightarrow V\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=C(\phi;\psi)^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\big\},

(103)

where the latter is the space of
twisted
equivariant function on
\Phi
.
As we highlighted in section
2.2.2
, neglecting the action of
\Diff(M)
on the integration domain, the action functional
S
of a theory defines a twisted equivariant function
Z=\exp(iS)
on
\Phi
– i.e. a section of a twisted associated (
\mathbb{C}
-line) bundle. See
[
Francois2019_II
;
Francois2021
;
Francois-et-al2021
]
.
In this paper, we adopt the more natural stance taking into account the natural action of
\Diff(M)
on integration domains,
17
17

17

Under which quantum functionals
Z=\int\boldsymbol{d}\phi\,\exp(\tfrac{i}{\hbar}S)
remains twisted equivariant, i.e. sections of a twisted
\mathbb{C}
-bundle. See footnote
11
.
see below.
The vertical transformation of such twisted equivariant functions is given by (
57
)-(
61
).
A twisted connection as discussed in section
2.4.2
is needed for their covariant differentiation.

2.5.1
Associated bundle of regions

There is a particular bundle,
\boldsymbol{E}=\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)
, canonically associated to
\Phi
, as it is built via the
defining representation
of its structure group
\Diff(M)
:
18
18

18

In that respect it is the conceptual equivalent of e.g.
TM
for the frame bundle
LM
of
M
. The tangent bundle can indeed be seen as associated to
LM
via the defining representation
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of its structure group
GL(n)
:
TM=LM\times_{GL(n)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

the field (or
\sigma
-algebra) of open sets of
M
,
\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{U}(M)\vcentcolon=\big\{U\subset M\,|\,U\text{ open set}\big\}
.
19
19

19

Purists will notice that this highlights the fact that
\Diff(M)
is a (Lie) pseudo-group rather than a (Lie) group. See e.g.
[
Kobayashi1972
]
. Indeed, one may aim to generalise the whole formalism presented here to pseudo-groups, or even groupoids.

The right action of
\Diff(M)
on the product space
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
is,

\displaystyle\big(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)\big)\times\Diff(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M),

(104)

\displaystyle\big((\phi,U),\psi\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bar{R}_{\psi}(\phi,U)\vcentcolon=\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\psi^{-1}(U)\big).

Notice the right action on
\boldsymbol{U}(M)
,
R_{\psi}U=\psi^{-1}(U)
(preimage convention), instead of the familiar defining left action
L_{\psi}U=\psi(U)
(direct image convention), is not accidental here but required by the bundle geometry framework (where the right action convention is now settled standard practice).
The associated bundle is thus:

\displaystyle\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)=\Phi\times_{\text{{\tiny$\Diff(M)$}}}\boldsymbol{U}(M)\vcentcolon=\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)/\!\sim.

(105)

Call it the “
associated bundle of regions
” of
M
.
Its space of sections
\Gamma\big(\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)\big)\vcentcolon=\big\{\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)\big\}
is isomorphic to

\displaystyle\Omega^{0}_{\text{eq}}\big(\Phi,U(M)\big)\vcentcolon=\big\{\boldsymbol{U}:\Phi\rightarrow\boldsymbol{U}(M)\,|\,R^{\star}_{\psi}{\boldsymbol{U}}=\psi^{-1}({\boldsymbol{U}})\big\}.

(106)

We may interpret
\phi\rightarrow\boldsymbol{U}(\phi)
as being “field-dependent” open sets of
M
, or yet, regions of
M
defined in a “
\phi
-
relative
” – and
\Diff(M)
-equivariant – way.
Their transformations under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
are respectively, by (
57
)-(
61
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{U}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{U}=-\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{U}).

(107)

An example of such equivariant functions is provided by a familiar operation: integration.
To show this, in the next section, we frame integration as a special case of a natural construction over the product space
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
.

2.5.2
Integration map

To obtain an associated bundle, we defined above a right action of
\Diff(M)
on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
:
\bar{R}_{\psi}(\phi,v)\vcentcolon=\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\big)
.
Let us now also define a corresponding action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
:

\displaystyle\big(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V},

(108)

\displaystyle\big((\phi,v),\boldsymbol{\psi}\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\phi,\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\big)=\big(\Xi(\phi),\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\big)=\vcentcolon\bar{\Xi}(\phi,v).

It is easily checked that in particular, for
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
, we have
\bar{\Xi}\boldsymbol{\circ}\bar{R}_{\psi}=\bar{R}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\circ}\bar{\Xi}
.
The action of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
may be seen as the linearisation of the above,

\big(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\rightarrow V(\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V})\simeq V\Phi\oplus V\boldsymbol{V}\subset T(P\times\boldsymbol{V})
,
which is as (
102
) under the replacement
X\rightarrow\boldsymbol{X}
.

The induced actions of
\Diff(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
on the space
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\times\boldsymbol{V}
are

\displaystyle\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times\Diff(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\times\boldsymbol{V},

(109)

\displaystyle\big((\boldsymbol{\alpha},v),\psi\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\big)=\vcentcolon\tilde{R}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v).

and

\displaystyle\big(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\times\boldsymbol{V}\big)\times C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\times\boldsymbol{V},

(110)

\displaystyle\big((\boldsymbol{\alpha},v),\boldsymbol{\psi}\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\big)=\vcentcolon\tilde{\Xi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v).

The induced actions of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
are the linearisations:

\displaystyle\big((\boldsymbol{\alpha},v),X\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\tilde{R}_{\psi_{\tau}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\big(\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\big)\oplus\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(X)v\big),

(111)

\displaystyle\big((\boldsymbol{\alpha},v),\boldsymbol{X}\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\tilde{\Xi}_{\tau}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\big(\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\big)\oplus\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})v\big)

We further observe that given a representation
(\tilde{\rho},\boldsymbol{W})
of
\Diff(M)
:

\displaystyle\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{W})\ \text{ then }\ \tilde{R}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)=\big(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\big)=\big(\tilde{\rho}(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\big),

(112)

\displaystyle\text{if }\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{W})\ \text{ then }\ \tilde{\Xi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)=\big(\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\big)=\big(\tilde{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\big),

Linear versions are obviously read from (
111
).
Remark that the exterior derivative
\boldsymbol{d}
on
\Phi
a priori extends to
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
as
{\boldsymbol{d}}\rightarrow\bar{\boldsymbol{d}}=\boldsymbol{d}\times\text{id}
, which we may still write
\boldsymbol{d}
for simplicity. Yet, following the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
, it will also act on the second factor
\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v
due to the
\phi
-dependence of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

Consider
(\bar{\rho},\boldsymbol{V}^{*})
a representation of
\Diff(M)

dual
to
(\rho,\boldsymbol{V})
w.r.t. a non-degenerate
\Diff(M)
-invariant
pairing

\displaystyle\langle\ ,\ \rangle:\boldsymbol{V}^{*}\times\boldsymbol{V}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

(113)

\displaystyle(w,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle w,v\rangle,\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\langle\,\bar{\rho}(\psi)w,\rho(\psi)v\rangle=\langle w,v\rangle.

Invariance of the pairing means that under
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
, for which we have the induced representation
\bar{\rho}_{*}
and
\rho_{*}
, the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\langle\,\bar{\rho}_{*}(X)w,v\rangle+\langle\,w,\rho_{*}(X)v\rangle=0

(114)

For
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V}^{*})
, let us then define the operation
\mathcal{I}
on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V}^{*})\times\boldsymbol{V}
by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V}^{*})\times\boldsymbol{V}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi),

(115)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)\vcentcolon=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},v\rangle.

Which can also be seen as an object on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ ):\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda^{\bullet}(\Phi),

(116)

\displaystyle(\phi,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},v)\vcentcolon=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},v\rangle.

Naturally, we thus have:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{d\alpha},\ ),\quad\text{ and }\quad\iota_{\mathfrak{X}}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )=\mathcal{I}(\iota_{\mathfrak{X}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )\quad\text{ for }\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi).

(117)

The induced actions of
\Diff(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
on such an object are:

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{\star}_{\psi}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|(\psi^{*}\phi,\ \rho(\psi)^{-1}v)}\vcentcolon=\langle\ ,\ \rangle\circ\tilde{R}_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)=\langle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\psi^{*}\phi},\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\rangle,

(118)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\ )_{|(\Xi(\phi),\ \rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v)}\vcentcolon=\langle\ ,\ \rangle\circ\tilde{\Xi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)=\langle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle.

The induced actions of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
are thus (with simplified but obvious notation):

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\tilde{R}_{\psi_{\tau}}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(X)v\rangle,

(119)

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\tilde{\Xi}_{\tau}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},v)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})v\rangle.

We then observe that, if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V}^{*})
:

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{\star}_{\psi}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|(\psi^{*}\phi,\ \rho(\psi)^{-1}v)}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\langle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\psi^{*}\phi},\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\rangle,

(120)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\rho}(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},\,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},v\rangle=\vcentcolon\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|(\phi,v)}.

From which follows, by (
119
), the identity:

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(X)v\rangle

\displaystyle=0,\qquad X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M).

(121)

\displaystyle\langle-\bar{\rho}_{*}(X)\,\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(X)v\rangle

\displaystyle=0

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V}^{*})
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|(\Xi(\phi),\ \rho(\psi)^{-1}v)}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\langle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle,

(122)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\bar{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},v\rangle=\vcentcolon\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|(\phi,v)}.

From which follows, by (
119
), the identity:

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})v\rangle

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big).

(123)

\displaystyle\langle-\bar{\rho}_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})\,\alpha,\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{X})v\rangle

\displaystyle=0

This last case,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
tensorial, ensures that
\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )
is “basic” on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
, thus well-defined as an object on
\boldsymbol{E}=\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}/\sim
.
Being constant along a
\Diff(M)
-orbit in
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{V}
,
\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )
then allows to define

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\!\in\Omega^{0}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi,\rho)
via the prescription:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\phi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\pi_{\boldsymbol{V}}(\phi,v)_{|\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},v)=cst}\equiv v,

(124)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}(\psi^{*}\phi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\pi_{\boldsymbol{V}}(\psi^{*}\phi,\rho(\psi)^{-1}v)_{|\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},v)=cst}\equiv\rho(\psi)^{-1}v.

Which in turn is equivalent to a section
\boldsymbol{s}_{\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{E}
, by (
100
).

We may also observe that (
122
) implies that for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,\boldsymbol{V}^{*})
one has

\boldsymbol{d}\,\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )=\boldsymbol{d}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{d\alpha},\ )
.
This is shown explicitly using Lemma (
74
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\,\langle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d}\,\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle\ +\ \langle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\boldsymbol{d}\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle,

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d}\,\bar{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle\ +\ \langle\bar{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\boldsymbol{d}\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle,

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\bar{\rho}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big),\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle\ +\ \langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})\boldsymbol{d}\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle,

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\bar{\rho}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle\ +\ \langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\psi}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\,v\rangle,

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha},v\rangle+\underbrace{\langle-\bar{\rho}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\,v\rangle}_{=0\,\text{ by \eqref{infinitesimal-equiv-pairing}}},

\displaystyle\text{i.e.}\quad\boldsymbol{d}\,\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{d\alpha},\ )=\boldsymbol{d}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ ).

(125)

Noticing that the first term on the right-hand side of the first line above is (stretching a bit the notation for simplicity)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{d}\,\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle=\langle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}v\rangle=\vcentcolon\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},v\rangle,

the above computation also supplies the following identity:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},v\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})v\rangle,

(126)

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},v\rangle+\langle\bar{\rho}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha},v\rangle.

Let us specialise the above construction, considering the “fundamental” representation
\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{U}(M)
on which
\Diff(M)
acts via the preimage convention, and the representation
\boldsymbol{V}^{*}=\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)
of volume forms on
U\in\boldsymbol{U}(M)
on which
\Diff(M)
acts by pullback. These are dual under the invariant
integration pairing
:

\displaystyle\langle\ ,\ \rangle:\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\text{top}}(U)\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

(127)

\displaystyle(\omega,U)

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle\omega,U\rangle\vcentcolon=\int_{U}\omega.

The invariance property assumes the familiar form:

\displaystyle\langle\,\psi^{*}\omega,\psi^{-1}(U)\rangle=\langle\omega,U\rangle\quad\rightarrow\quad\int_{\psi^{-1}(U)}\psi^{*}\omega=\int_{U}\omega.

(128)

This, as a special case of (
114
) with
-\bar{\rho}_{*}(X)=\mathfrak{L}_{X}
and
-\rho_{*}(X)=-X
, gives the identity:

\displaystyle\langle\,\mathfrak{L}_{X}\omega,U\rangle+\langle\,\omega,-X(U)\rangle=0\quad\rightarrow\quad\int_{U}\mathfrak{L}_{X}\omega+\int_{-X(U)}\hskip-5.69054pt\omega=0,

(129)

which amounts to a continuity equation for the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
.
The form is reminiscent of Stokes theorem, i.e. the duality between the DeRham derivative
d
on
\Omega^{\bullet}(U)
and the boundary operator
\partial
on
\boldsymbol{U}(M)
, adjoint operators w.r.t. to the integration pairing:

\displaystyle\langle\,d\omega,U\rangle=\langle\,\omega,\partial U\rangle\rightarrow\quad\int_{U}d\omega=\int_{\partial U}\hskip-5.69054pt\omega,

(130)

degree and dimension being appropriately adjusted.

Considering
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)
, the field-dependent volume forms, we define the integration map on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},U)=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},U\rangle\vcentcolon=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi}.

(131)

We will sometimes use the simplified notation
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}
when more convenient.
We have naturally
\boldsymbol{d}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{d\alpha},U)
, and
\iota_{\mathfrak{X}}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)=\mathcal{I}(\iota_{\mathfrak{X}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)
for
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
– as a special case of (
117
).
The induced actions of
\Diff(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
on integrals are:

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{\star}_{\psi}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\ \psi^{-1}(U)\big)}\vcentcolon=\langle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\psi^{*}\phi},\,\psi^{-1}(U)\rangle=\int_{\psi^{-1}(U)}R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\psi^{*}\phi}

(132)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Xi}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\ )_{|\big(\Xi(\phi),\ \boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)\big)}\vcentcolon=\langle\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)},\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)}\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\Xi(\phi)}.

(133)

One may notice that, in the latter case, the derivative
\boldsymbol{d}
will now act also on the transformed region
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)
due to the
\phi
-dependence of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.
Using the notation just mentioned, we may write the above as
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\psi}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
respectively.
The induced actions of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
and
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)
on integrals are thus (with simplified notation):

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\tilde{R}_{\psi_{\tau}}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,U\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-X(U)\rangle=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\int_{-X(U)}\boldsymbol{\alpha},

(134)

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,\tilde{\Xi}_{\tau}^{\star}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)\,\big|_{\tau=0}=\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,U\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\boldsymbol{X}(U)\rangle=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\int_{-\boldsymbol{X}(U)}\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

When convenient, we may write the above as
\delta_{X}\ \!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}
and
\delta_{\boldsymbol{X}}\ \!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}
respectively.

In case
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is tensorial on
\Phi
, we have the special case of the results (
122
) above, so that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{|\phi},U)
is
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
-invariant:
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}
.
From which follows, as a special case of (
123
) and (
134
), that:

\displaystyle\delta_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\langle\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,U\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\boldsymbol{X}(U)\rangle

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\boldsymbol{X}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big).

(135)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\alpha,\,U\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\,-\boldsymbol{X}(U)\rangle

\displaystyle=0\quad\rightarrow\quad\int_{U}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\alpha+\int_{-\boldsymbol{X}(U)}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0.

This can be interpreted as a continuity equations or sort. For
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
equivariant, we have
\Diff(M)
-invariance of its integral:
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\psi}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}
, and (
135
) holds mutatis mutandis (
\boldsymbol{X}\rightarrow X
).
For
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
tensorial,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)
is thus well-defined on the bundle of regions
\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)=\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)/\sim
, and one may – quite formally still – define an equivariant
\boldsymbol{U}(M)
-valued function on
\Phi
as in (
124
).
It also mean that
\boldsymbol{d}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}})=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\,\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle=\boldsymbol{d}\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle\quad\rightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{d}\int_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}=\boldsymbol{d}\int_{U}\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

(136)

Which is also proven in exactly the same way as (
125
), using the special form (
75
) of the lemma (
74
) and concluding by the invariance property (
129
).
Also, specialising (
126
) we get the identity:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{(}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}_{U})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d\alpha}_{U}+\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle,

(137)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},U\rangle^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},U\rangle+\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle.

These are relevant to the variational principle.

Indeed, consider a Lagrangian
L\in\Omega^{0}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)
, a tensorial 0-form on
\Phi
and volume form on
U
.
The corresponding action functional is the invariant object on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
:
S=\mathcal{I}(L,U)=\langle L,U\rangle=\int_{U}L
,
or yet
S(\phi)=\mathcal{I}\big(L(\phi),U\big)=\langle L(\phi),U\rangle=\int_{U}L(\phi)
, and s.t.
S^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\langle L^{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}L,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\langle L,U\rangle=S
.
The variational principle is expressed as
\boldsymbol{d}S=\boldsymbol{d}\mathcal{I}(L,U)=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{d}L,U)=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{d}L\equiv 0
.
Under the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
, we have on the one hand by (
136
) that
\boldsymbol{d}(S^{\boldsymbol{\psi}})=\boldsymbol{d}S
, from which follows as a special case of (
135
) that
\delta_{\boldsymbol{X}}S=0
, i.e. the relation
\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}L,U\rangle+\langle L,-\boldsymbol{X}(U)\rangle=0
.
On the other hand, by (
137
) above, we have the relation:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{d}S)^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}S+\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}L,U\rangle

(138)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}S+\langle d\iota_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}L,U\rangle=\boldsymbol{d}S+\langle\iota_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}L,\partial U\rangle.

The fact that
\boldsymbol{d}S
and its transform differ by a boundary term implies that the variational principle remain well-defined under the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
, and that the space of solution
\mathcal{S}
is stable under field-dependent transformations.
20
20

20

As will be shown more explicitly in section
, when we obtain the transformation of the field equations (
).

This, in other words, proves that
\Diff(M)
-covariant theories
L
enjoy a larger
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
-covariance – a fact pointed out in the particular case of GR by Bergmann and Komar in 1971
[
Bergmann-Komar1972
]
.

The equivariant
\boldsymbol{U}(M)
-valued function associated to
S
is:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{S}(\phi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\pi_{\boldsymbol{U}(M)}(\phi,U)_{|S=cst}=U,

(139)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{S}(\psi^{*}\phi)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\pi_{\boldsymbol{U}(M)}\big(\psi^{*}\phi,\psi^{-1}(U)\big)_{|S=cst}=\psi^{-1}(U).

Its
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
transformations are thus respectively, by (
57
)-(
61
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{S}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{S}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{U}_{S}=-\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{U}_{S}).

(140)

The function outputs the region on which the fields
\phi
, and volume form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\phi)=L(\phi)
, are defined and integrated over.
So, it subverts the usual logic by making the region
U
individuated by the fields it hosts. This is not surprising since the viewpoint articulated here,“dual” to the usual one, reframes familiar geometric objects in a way that gives primacy to the bundle of fields
\Phi
, and sees regions as representation space associated to
\Phi
and its transformation groups.
This, we may say, is a way to define regions of
M
in a “
\phi
-
relative
” and
\Diff(M)
-
equivariant
way. As we will see in section
3
next, the DFM will allow a step further, suggesting a way to define regions of
spacetime
in a
\phi
-
relational
and
\Diff(M)
-
invariant
way – in accordance with the key insight of general relativistic physics.

3
The dressing field method

We now turn the main topic of this paper.
The dressing field method (DFM) is a systematic algorithm to build basic forms on a bundle, i.e. to obtain gauge-invariants in gauge theories.
It was developed primarily for internal gauge groups, i.e. for Yang-Mills theories and gauge gravity theories formulated via Cartan geometry
[
GaugeInvCompFields
;
Francois2014
;
Attard_et_al2017
;
Attard-Francois2016_II
;
Attard-Francois2016_I
;
Francois2018
;
Berghofer-et-al2023
]
.
Relatively complete and self-contained expositions can be found in
[
Francois2021
;
Francois-et-al2021
]
.

In this section we detail the natural extension of the DFM to theories with
\Diff(M)
symmetry.
As the next sections will make manifest, it is the general unifying geometric framework for the notions of
gravitational edge modes
as introduced in
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]
and further developed e.g. in
[
Speranza2018
;
Chandrasekaran_Speranza2021
;
Speranza2022
;
Chandrasekaran-et-al2022
]
, of
gravitational dressings
as proposed in
[
Giddings-Donnelly2016
;
Giddings-Donnelly2016-bis
;
Giddings-Kinsella2018
;
Giddings2019
;
Giddings-Weinberg2020
]
– see also
[
Kabel-Wieland2022
]
– as well as the more recent idea of
“dynamical reference frames” as expounded in
[
carrozza-et-al2022
;
Hoehn-et-al2022
]
, or yet that of “embedding maps/fields” as advocated in
[
Speranza2019
;
Ciambelli-et-al2022
;
Speranza2022
;
Ciambelli2023
]
.

But a more fundamental fact, we will argue, is that the DFM is a framework that formally makes manifest the
relational
character of general relativistic physics:
It does so by systematically implementing a notion of
relational observables
, or
Dirac variables
, for
\Diff(M)
-theories
[
Tamborino2012
]
.

3.1
Building basic forms via dressing

One may profitably compare what follows to the DFM for theories with internal gauge group as presented in
[
Francois2021
;
Francois-et-al2021
]
.
One defines the space of
\Diff(M)
-dressing fields as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}r[N,M]\vcentcolon=\left\{\ u:N\rightarrow M\ |\ u^{\psi}\vcentcolon=\psi^{-1}\circ u,\ \text{ with }\ \psi\in\Diff(M)\right\},

(141)

where
N
is a reference
n
-dim manifold. From this, the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
in the dressing field
u
must be
\delta_{X}u\vcentcolon=-X\circ u
. Given a field
\phi\in\Phi
in
M
, acted upon by
\Diff(M)
as
R_{\psi}=\psi^{*}\phi
, the dressing map is defined as:

\displaystyle|^{u}:\Phi\

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ \Phi^{u},

\displaystyle\phi\

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \phi^{u}\vcentcolon=\ u^{*}\phi.

(142)

Clearly,
\phi^{u}
, called the
dressing
of
\phi
, is
\Diff(M)
-invariant:
(u^{*}\phi)^{\psi}\vcentcolon=(u^{\psi})^{*}(\phi^{\psi})=(\psi^{-1}\circ u)^{*}(\psi^{*}\phi)=u^{*}\phi
. Which is unsurprising as the space
\Phi^{u}
of dressed fields is a subset of fields living on
N
.

More generally, one defines a
field-dependent dressing field
as a smooth map,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}:\Phi\

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ \mathcal{D}r[N,M]

\displaystyle\phi\

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \boldsymbol{u}(\phi),\qquad\text{ s.t. }\quad R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}=\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}\quad\text{ i.e. }\quad\boldsymbol{u}(\phi^{\psi})=\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi).

(143)

In other words,
\boldsymbol{u}
is an equivariant 0-form on
\Phi
–
\mathcal{D}r[N,M]
being a representation space for
\Diff(M)
. Its infinitesimal equivariance, i.e.
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
-transformation, is then
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{u}=-X\circ\boldsymbol{u}
.
And its transformations under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{diff}}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
are respectively:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u^{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{u}=-\boldsymbol{X}\circ\boldsymbol{u}.

(144)

Such a
\phi
-dependent dressing field allows to define the map

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{u}}:\Phi\

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ \mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\phi\

\displaystyle\mapsto\ F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi\sim[\phi],\qquad\text{ s.t. }\quad F_{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ R_{\psi}=F_{\boldsymbol{u}}.

(145)

Clearly,
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi^{\psi})=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi^{\psi})^{*}(\phi^{\psi})=\big(\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\big)^{*}\psi^{*}\phi=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi=\vcentcolon F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)
. This means that the image of
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}
is a “coordinatisation” of
\mathcal{M}
since each
\Diff(M)
-orbit
[\phi]\in\mathcal{M}
of
\phi\in\Phi
is represented by a tensor field
\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi
on
N
(rather than on
M
).
A very natural understanding of the dressed field
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi
, is that it is a
relational variable
. Indeed, being
\Diff(M)
-invariant it represents the physical d.o.f. of a theory, but it also does so in a manifestly
relational
way: the very expression
\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi
is explicitly a field-dependent coordinatisation of the physical d.o.f., i.e. a definition of physical d.o.f. with respect to each other.
The dressed fields
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
are thus Dirac relational variables, or “complete observables” in the terminology of
[
Rovelli2002
;
Tamborino2012
]
. And as we are about to see, the DFM is then a framework allowing to systematically reformulate a theory in a manifestly
\Diff(M)
-invariant and relational way.

Notice that the map (
3.1
) is a realisation of the bundle projection,
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}\sim\pi
.
Therefore, it becomes possible in principle to build basic forms on
\Phi
since, as seen in section
2.2.2
,

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{Basic}}(\Phi)=
Im
\,\pi^{\star}\simeq
Im
\,F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}
.
Given a form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi\big)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
, to build its basic counterpart we first consider its formal “version” on the base space,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}[\phi];[\phi]\big)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
, then simply define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\vcentcolon=F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\bar{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}[\phi];F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)\big)\quad\in\ \Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{Basic}}(\Phi).

(146)

This object, basic by definition, is called the
dressing
of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. By construction it is invariant under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
– thus also gauge-invariant, under
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
:
(\boldsymbol{\alpha^{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha^{u}}
.

To get a more operational expression for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
, one needs only to find the explicit results for
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]
which is a basis for basic forms.
This is done by first finding the general result of the pushforward
{F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}:T_{\phi}\Phi\rightarrow T_{F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)}\mathcal{M}
,
\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\mapsto{F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}
. Indeed, given a generic
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
with flow
\varphi_{\tau}:\Phi\rightarrow\Phi
, s.t.
\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big|_{\tau=0}=\mathfrak{X}(\phi)\tfrac{\delta}{\delta\phi}
, one has
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)}\big(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\big)=\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)}\big({F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\big)
. So,

\displaystyle{F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\vcentcolon={F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\ \big|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ {F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\ \big|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)^{*}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\ \big|_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)^{*}\phi\ \big|_{\tau=0}+\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\ \big|_{\tau=0}

The first contribution is, inserting
\text{id}_{N}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
,

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}^{{}_{\text{\scriptsize$*$}}}\boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)^{*}\phi\ \big|_{\tau=0}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \big(\boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}\big)^{*}\phi\ \big|_{\tau=0}.

The term
\boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}
is a curve in
M
, so
\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}\ \big|_{\tau=0}=\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}\ \in\Gamma(TM)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)^{*}\phi\ \big|_{\tau=0}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \big(\boldsymbol{u}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}\big)^{*}\phi\ \big|_{\tau=0}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}\phi.

(147)

The second contribution is

\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\ \big|_{\tau=0}=\vcentcolon\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\ \big|_{\tau=0}=F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\,\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}
,
where
\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
is considered
\phi
-constant/ independent. Now, this is indeed a vector (field) on
\mathcal{M}
, so we can find its expression as a derivation by applying it to
\boldsymbol{g}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
:

\displaystyle\big[F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\,\star}\mathfrak{X}\,\big(\boldsymbol{g}\big)\big]([\phi])

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\ \boldsymbol{g}\left(F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\right)\ \big|_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{\delta}{\delta[\phi]}\boldsymbol{g}\right)\big(F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}(\phi)\big)\ \underbrace{\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}\big(\varphi_{\tau}(\phi)\big)\,\big|_{\tau=0}}_{\big[\mathfrak{X}(F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)})\big](\phi)}=\left(\tfrac{\delta}{\delta[\phi]}\boldsymbol{g}\right)\big(\underbrace{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi}_{\sim[\phi]}\big)\ \underbrace{\big(\tfrac{\delta}{\delta\phi}F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}\big)(\phi)}_{\tfrac{\delta}{\delta\phi}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi\,=\,\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}}\ \mathfrak{X}(\phi),

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{\delta}{\delta[\phi]}\boldsymbol{g}\right)\big([\phi]\big)\ \boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\mathfrak{X}(\phi)=\big[\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\mathfrak{X}(\phi)\tfrac{\delta}{\delta[\phi]}(\boldsymbol{g})\big]([\phi]).

Then, gathering the two contributions, we have

\displaystyle{F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\left(\mathfrak{X}(\phi)+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}\phi\right)\,\frac{\delta}{\delta[\phi]}_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)(\phi)}}.

(148)

From this we deduce, for any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Gamma(T\Phi)
:

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)(\phi)}}\,\big(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)(\phi)}}\,\big({F_{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\star}\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\big),
