\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)(\phi)}}\,\left(\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\left(\mathfrak{X}(\phi)+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}\phi\right)\,\tfrac{\delta}{\delta[\phi]}_{|F_{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)(\phi)}}\right),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\left(\mathfrak{X}(\phi)+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}\phi\right),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi}\,\big(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}\big)+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}\phi\right),

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi_{|\phi}+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}}\phi\big)\right)(\mathfrak{X}_{|\phi}).

We thus obtain the dressing of
\boldsymbol{d}\phi
, the basic basis 1-form:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\vcentcolon=F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}[\phi]=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}\phi\big)\quad\in\ \Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi),

(149)

where
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
. This can be compared e.g. to eq.(3.30) in
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]
, eq.(2.7) in
[
Speranza2022
]
, or eq.(2.8) in
[
Geiller2018
]
.
The result (
149
) can then be inserted into (
146
) to get the dressing of any form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}};\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\big)\ \ \in\ \Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{Basic}}(\Phi),

(150)

which is basic by construction, thus invariant under
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
– and under
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
.

Given the formal similarity between
\Xi(\phi)=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\phi)^{*}\phi
and
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{*}\phi
on the one hand, and between
\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
(
66
) and
\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
(
149
) on the other hand, which results into the formal similarity between
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
(
43
) and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
(
146
)-(
150
), we can spell out the following rule of thumb to obtain the dressing of any form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
:
First compute its
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
transformation
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
, then substitute
\boldsymbol{\psi}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}
in the resulting expression to obtain
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
.
We will use this rule of thumb from now on.

Let us illustrate it by discussing the case of the dressing of twisted tensorial forms. For this to be well defined, one needs only to assume that the 1-cocycle (
46
),
C:\Phi\times\Diff(M)\rightarrow G
, controlling the equivariance (
47
) of these forms has a functional expression s.t. is can be meaningfully extended to
C:\Phi\times\Diff(N,M)\rightarrow G
. If so, the map

\displaystyle C(\boldsymbol{u}):\Phi

\displaystyle\rightarrow G,

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto[C(\boldsymbol{u})](\phi)\vcentcolon=C\big(\phi;\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\big),

(151)

is well-defined and is a twisted equivariant 0-form:

\displaystyle\big[R^{\star}_{\psi}C(\boldsymbol{u})\big](\phi)

\displaystyle=\big[C(\boldsymbol{u})\circ R_{\psi}\big](\phi)\vcentcolon=C\big(\phi^{\psi};\boldsymbol{u}(\phi^{\psi})\big)=C\big(\phi^{\psi};\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\big)=C\big(\phi^{\psi};\psi^{-1}\big)\cdot C\big(\phi;\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\big),

\displaystyle=C\big(\phi;\psi\big)^{-1}\!\cdot C\big(\phi;\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\big),

\displaystyle=\vcentcolon\big[C\big(\ \,;\psi\big)^{-1}\!\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u})\big](\phi),

(152)

where the cocycle property (
46
) is used.
The linearisation is
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}C(\boldsymbol{u})=\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{u})=-a(X;\ \,)\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u})
.
In other words, it is a dressing field for twisted forms. It follows that, as a special case of (
57
), its
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
transformation is,

\displaystyle C(\boldsymbol{u})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\!\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u}).

(153)

Applying e.g. the rule of thumb to
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,C)
, by (
57
) we know that its
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
-transformation is
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, from which we read its dressing to be

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=C(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\ \in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi).

(154)

It is by construction invariant under vertical transformations
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
, thus also under gauge transformations
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
: which in this case is easily checked using (
153
),

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\big(C(\boldsymbol{u})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\big(C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\!\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u})\big)^{-1}\!\cdot C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=C(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}.

(155)

Of course this specialises to the case of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,\rho)
, so
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Taking the case of an Ehresmann connection, in view of (
71
) our rule of thumb ensures that
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{\omega}\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{du}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi)
. This allows us to write an analogue of the lemma (
74
)-(
75
), also occasionally useful:
For
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{D\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}(\Phi,\rho)
,
we have on the one hand
\boldsymbol{d}\,F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\bar{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\boldsymbol{d}\big(\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)
. On the other hand, since
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}+\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{D\alpha}
(by our rule of thumb again), we have that,

\displaystyle F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}\boldsymbol{d}\bar{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{D\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}+\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\,\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u})\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha},

\displaystyle=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{d\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right).

By naturality of the pullback
[F_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star},\boldsymbol{d}]=0
(understood that the exterior derivatives belong to different spaces here) we obtain:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\big(\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{d\alpha}-\rho_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1})\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right)

(156)

In particular, for the pullback representation
\rho(u)^{-1}\!=u^{*}
, this is:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\big)=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{d\alpha}+\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right).

(157)

The latter identity appears in the covariant phase space and edge mode literature, e.g.
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
;
Speranza2018
;
Kabel-Wieland2022
]
.
It must not be conflated with with (
149
) as the two results have distinct geometric origins and meaning.

3.1.1
Dressing field and flat connections

Given a field-dependent dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}
, the object
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\vcentcolon=-\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi)
is a
flat Ehresmann connection
. Indeed, on the one hand, by (
144
) we have,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}}_{|\phi}(X^{v}_{|\phi})=-\boldsymbol{du}_{|\phi}(X^{v}_{|\phi})\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}=-\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}=X\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)^{-1}=X\ \in\mathfrak{diff}(M).

(158)

On the other hand, by definition (
3.1
) and using the naturality of
\boldsymbol{d}
(commuting with pullbacks),

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}

\displaystyle=-R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{du}\circ(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}=-\boldsymbol{d}(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u})\circ(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}=-\boldsymbol{d}(\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u})\circ(\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}=\psi^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}\circ\psi,

\displaystyle=\psi^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\!\circ\psi.

(159)

These are indeed the defining properties (
68
) of an Ehresmann connection. It is immediate to see that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}+\tfrac{1}{2}[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}}\equiv 0,

(160)

i.e. that a dressing field supplies a
flat
connection on
\Phi
.
An immediate corollary is that,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d\psi},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}=\boldsymbol{dX}+[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}},\boldsymbol{X}]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{diff}(M)$}}\,},

(161)

as a special case of (
71
) and (
72
). This may be compared to eq.(3.19) in
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]
.

Thus, (
149
) can also be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}}\phi\big)\quad\in\ \Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi).

(162)

Being basic by construction, we know it is
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
-invariant (and also gauge-invariant, under
\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi)
), but let us check it as an exercise: Using (
66
), (
144
) and (
164
), we have

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{u}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}})^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\big),

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u})^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi+\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}]}\phi\big)-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d\psi}}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\phi\right),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}^{{}_{\text{\scriptsize$*$}}}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\phi+\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}]}\phi)\ -\ \boldsymbol{u}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}^{{}_{\text{\scriptsize$*$}}}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\phi\ -\ \boldsymbol{u}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}}^{{}_{\text{\scriptsize$*$}}}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d\psi}}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\phi,

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\boldsymbol{d}\phi+\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}]}\,\phi\ -\ \boldsymbol{u}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}}\,\phi\ -\ \boldsymbol{u}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\psi}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}]}\,\phi,

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}}\phi\big)=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}.

Where the lemma (
2.1.1
) is used in the 3rd to 4th line.
Now, given (
162
), for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha(\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi)\,\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
, (
150
) may be written,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\alpha\big(\boldsymbol{u}^{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\phi-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}});\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\big)=\alpha\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi-\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}};\phi\big)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\alpha\big(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}};\phi\big),

(163)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\iota_{[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(\ )]^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\quad\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi).

The formulae (
162
) and (
163
) may be compared to (
78
) and (
77
)/(
80
): It shows that using the DFM or Ehresmann connections to build a basic counterparts of invariant 1-forms,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
, gives formally very analogous results.
21
21

21

An observation first hinted at in
[
Gomes-et-al2018
]
in the context of the study of the covariant phase space approach to field theory (over bounded regions) with internal gauge symmetries: there
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
is the presymplectic potential of the theory. See also
[
Francois-et-al2021
]
for a detailed discussion of this point for this class of theories.
More on this in section
3.2.2
below.

This also tells us something noteworthy: The existence of a flat connection, hence of a global dressing field, is a strong topological constraint on a bundle.
22
22

22

In the finite dimensional case of a bundle
P
over a simply connected manifold, it implies that
P
is trivial.
In some cases, a dressing field indeed gives a global trivialisation of the bundle
\Phi
, yet in general the field space may not be trivial and Gribov-like obstructions may exclude the existence of dressing fields other than local. A generic, non-flat, connection
\boldsymbol{\omega}
does not entail such constraints.

In the same way, a dressing field may supply a
flat twisted connection

\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\vcentcolon=-\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{u})\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}
, satisfying the defining properties (
88
) – as can be checked using (
3.1
) and
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}C(\boldsymbol{u})=-a(X;\ \,)\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u})
.
From which follows that, as a special case of (
94
)-(
95
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\text{Ad}\big(C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\big)\,\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}+C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{\psi}),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}=\boldsymbol{d}a(\boldsymbol{X})+[\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}},a(\boldsymbol{X})]_{\text{{\tiny$\mathfrak{g}$}}}.

(164)

Reprising the example of
Z=\exp{iS}\in\Omega^{0}_{eq}(\Omega,C)
, the flat twisted connection is
\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\vcentcolon=-\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{u})\cdot C(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}=i\int\boldsymbol{d}c(\ \,;\boldsymbol{u})
. Then we may notice that the associated twisted covariant derivative is
\bar{\boldsymbol{D}}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}Z\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d}Z+\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}Z=(i\boldsymbol{d}S+\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}})Z
, which implies, as special case of (
91
),

\displaystyle i\boldsymbol{d}S+\boldsymbol{\varpi}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}=i\boldsymbol{d}\int L+c(\ \,;\boldsymbol{u})\quad\in\ \Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi).

(165)

The object
L^{\prime}\vcentcolon=L+c(\ \,;\boldsymbol{u})
is what one may call a Wess-Zumino improved Lagrangian,
c(\ \,;\boldsymbol{u})
playing the role of the
Wess-Zumino-Witten counterterm
restoring gauge-invariance – see
[
Bertlmann
]
chap.4,
[
GockSchuck
]
chap.12, or
[
Bonora2023
]
chap.15.
Given the definition of the 1-cocycle
c(\ \,;\psi)\vcentcolon=\psi^{*}L-L
, it is clear that
L^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}L\vcentcolon=L^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\Omega^{0}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi)
, i.e. it is but the dressed Lagrangian one would obtain by applying directly the DFM (and its rule of thumb) to a Lagrangian
L\in\Omega^{0}_{\text{eq}}(\Phi)
with equivariance
R^{\star}_{\psi}L=\psi^{*}L
and therefore gauge transformation
L^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}L
. Such dressed Lagrangians are key objects of our subsequent discussions.

3.2
Residual symmetries

In the context of the DFM, two kinds of
residual
symmetries are to be discussed: The (genuine) residual symmetry coming from the elimination of only part of the original symmetry, and a “new” symmetry that may arise (in replacement of the eliminated one) due to possible ambiguities in the choice or contruction of a dressing field. Of course, both might arise simultaneously. Let us discuss them in turn.

3.2.1
Residual symmetries of the first kind

Consider
\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)\subset\Diff(M)
a subgroup of diffeomorphisms satisfying some condition (boundary conditions, compact support…).
And suppose the dressing field
\ \boldsymbol{u}:\Phi\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{D}r[N,M]\
has defining equivariance given by

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{u}=\varphi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u},\quad\text{for }\ \varphi\in\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M),

(166)

i.e.
\boldsymbol{u}(\varphi^{*}\phi)=\varphi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
.
Application of the DFM then amounts to building a bundle projection
\Phi\rightarrow\Phi/\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)
. For this to be a bundle map, i.e. for
\Phi/\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)=\vcentcolon\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
to be principal (sub)bundle in its own right, the quotient
\Diff(M)/\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)
must be a group, thus
\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)
needs to be a
normal
subgroup of
\Diff(M)
:
\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)\triangleleft\Diff(M)
.
Let us assume it is so,
23
23

23

Such is e.g. the group
\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)=\Diff_{\text{\tiny c}}(M)
of compactly supported diffeomorphisms.
and denote
\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\vcentcolon=\Diff(M)/\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)
the (residual) structure group of
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
.

Any dressed object
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
will then be
\Diff_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)
-basic on
\Phi
, i.e. invariant by construction under the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff_{\text{\tiny 0 }}(M)\big)\subset C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
– and
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(M)\subset\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
in particular.
But it is expected to exhibit
residual transformations
under
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\big)\subset C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
– and
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\subset\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)
in particular.
The transformation of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
under
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\big)
being known by assumption, that of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
boils down to determining the residual
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\big)
-transformation of the dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}
, itself controlled by its equivariance:
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}=?
for
\psi\in\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)
.

In that regard, there are two noteworthy cases that we can treat in a systematic way. Let us e.g. consider
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\rho\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathcal{C}
, whose dressing by
\boldsymbol{u}
as in (
166
) above are
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
,
thus
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff_{\text{\tiny 0 }}(M)\big)
-invariant.

Proposition 1
.

If
\ \boldsymbol{u}:\Phi\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{D}r[M,M]\
is s.t.

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}=\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}\circ\psi,\quad\text{ for }\ \psi\in\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M),

(167)

then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\Omega_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\rho\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore, their residual
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi;\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\big)
-transformations are:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d\psi}.

(168)

Indeed, firstly: It is easy to see that if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is horizontal, so is
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, and
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho\big(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}\big)^{-1}R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\rho\big(\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}\circ\psi\big)^{-1}\rho(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\rho(\psi^{-1})\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
.
Secondly, on the one hand, given the linear version of (
167
) is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{u}=\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{du}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}\boldsymbol{u}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=-X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{*}X,\quad\text{ for }\ X\vcentcolon=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\psi_{\tau}\,\big|_{\tau=0}\in\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M),

(169)

one has
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}(X^{v})=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{\omega}(X^{v})\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}(X^{v})=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\big(-X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{*}X\big)=X
.
Then, on the other hand, given (
167
) and (
68
), it is easy to show that:

R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\psi^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ\psi
, for
\psi\in\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)
.
So indeed,
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
satisfies the defining properties (
68
) of a
\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)
-principal connection.
The transformations (
168
) thus follow as a special case of (
57
) and (
71
).
From both follows in particular that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\Omega^{2}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\big)
, so
(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

The other interesting case is:

Proposition 2
.

If
\,\boldsymbol{u}:\Phi\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{D}r[N,M]\,
is s.t.

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}=\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}\circ C(\ \,;\psi),\quad\text{ for }\ \psi\in\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M),

(170)

and where
C:\Phi\times\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\rightarrow\Diff(N)
is a special case of 1-cocycle as defined in (
46
), thus satisfying the relation

C(\phi;\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)=C(\phi;\psi^{\prime})\circ C(\phi^{\psi^{\prime}};\psi)
.

Then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\Omega_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,C\big)
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Therefore, their residual
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)\big)
-transformations are:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\rho\big(C(\boldsymbol{\psi})\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ C(\boldsymbol{\psi})+C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}C(\boldsymbol{\psi}).

(171)

As above we have, firstly, that if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is horizontal, so is
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho(\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, and
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\rho\big(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}\big)^{-1}R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\rho\big(\psi^{-1}\!\circ\boldsymbol{u}\circ C(\ \,;\psi)\big)^{-1}\rho(\psi)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\rho\big(C(\ \,;\psi)\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
. That is, modulo
\rho
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
is a twisted tensorial form. Remark in particular that if
\rho
is the pullback action,
\rho(\psi)^{-1}=\psi^{*}
, then

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=C(\ \,;\psi)^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}},\quad\text{so}\quad(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})\big)^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}.

(172)

Secondly, on the one hand, given the linear version of (
170
) is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{u}=\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{du}=\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\,R^{\star}_{\psi_{\tau}}\boldsymbol{u}\,\big|_{\tau=0}=-X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{*}\,\tfrac{d}{d\tau}C(\ \,;\psi_{\tau})\,\big|_{\tau=0}=-X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{*}\,a(X;\ \,),\quad\text{ for }\ X\in\mathfrak{diff}_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M),

(173)

one has

\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}(X^{v})=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{\omega}(X^{v})\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}(X^{v})=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}_{*}\big(-X\circ\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{*}\,a(X;\ \,)\big)=a(X;\ \,)\ \in\mathfrak{diff}(N)
.
Then, on the other hand, given (
170
) and (
68
), it is easy to show that:

R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=C(\ \,;\psi)^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ C(\ \,;\psi)+C(\ \,;\psi)^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{d}C(\ \,;\psi)
, for
\psi\in\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)
.
So indeed,
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
satisfies the defining properties (
88
)-(
89
) of a
\Diff_{\text{\scriptsize r}}(M)
-twisted connection.
The transformations (
171
)-(
172
) thus follow as a special case of (
57
) and (
94
).
From both follows in particular that
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\Omega^{2}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,C\big)
, so
(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=C(\boldsymbol{\psi})^{-1}_{*}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\circ C(\boldsymbol{\psi})
.

We observe that Proposition
2
reproduces (up to left/right-pushforward/pullback convention) the construction of “relational observables” in section 3.4.4 of
[
Hoehn-et-al2022
]
– which we already noticed to be twisted tensorial 0-forms in section
2.4.2
: compare indeed
eq.(3.182) and eq. (3.185) to (
170
), then
eq.(3.183) and eq.(3.190) to (
172
). In their language, the field-dependent dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
is referred to as a “frame field” (noted
R[\phi]
).

We may also signal that exact analogues of Proposition
1
and
2
are used in the (finite dimensional) context of Cartan conformal geometry to build the twistor/tractor connection and twistor and tractor fields – in an application of the DFM for field theories with internal gauge groups
[
Attard-Francois2016_I
;
Attard-Francois2016_II
]
, see also
[
Attard_et_al2017
]
.
24
24

24

Considering the conformal Cartan geometry
(P,A)
, with
A
one among all conformal Cartan connections
\mathcal{A}
, one can build a
\mathcal{A}
-dependent dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}r(K,K)
,
A\mapsto\boldsymbol{u}(A)
, for
K
the (abelian) subgroup of special conformal transformations. The residual symmetry is
\mathcal{SO}(1,3)\times\mathcal{W}
, i.e. the Lorentz group and Weyl rescalings. The dressing satisfies Proposition
1
w.r.t. Lorentz, and Proposition
2
w.r.t. Weyl rescalings. Using it to dress the conformal Cartan connection
A
itself one gets
A^{\boldsymbol{u}}
, which is then a
mixed
connection: Ehresmann for
\mathcal{SO}(1,3)
and twisted for
\mathcal{W}
.
This mixed Cartan connection
A^{\boldsymbol{u}}
is none other than the twistor/tractor connection 1-form.
Conformal tractors and twistors are obtained by dressing sections of the associated
\mathbb{R}
- and
\mathbb{C}
-vector bundles
E
and
\sf E
respectively. See
[
Attard_et_al2017
;
Attard-Francois2016_I
;
Attard-Francois2016_II
]

3.2.2
Residual symmetries of the second kind

The second kind of residual symmetry, may arise even when the original
\Diff(M)
-symmetry has been entirely neutralised via DFM.
Indeed, if by definition of the dressing field
\,\boldsymbol{u}:\Phi\ \rightarrow\ \mathcal{D}r[N,M]\,
its equivariance
R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u}=\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u}
means that
\Diff(M)
acts on its target space as the map
\boldsymbol{u}(\phi):N\rightarrow M
, there may be a priori a natural right action of
\Diff(N)
on its source space:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}r[N,M]\times\Diff(N)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{D}r[N,M]

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{u},\varphi)

\displaystyle\mapsto\boldsymbol{u}\circ\varphi=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{u}^{\varphi}.

(174)

Another way to see it, is to remark that two candidate dressing fields
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{u}
can a priori be related by an element
\varphi\in\Diff(N)
acting on their common source space, the model manifold
N
:
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{u}\circ\varphi
. This indeed gives rise to the right action above,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\varphi}
.

Naturally,
\Diff(N)
does not act on
\Phi
, a fact we may note by:
\phi^{\varphi}=\phi
. But given that
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}=F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi)\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\phi
, it must be the case that
\Diff(N)
acts on the dressed variable via its action on the dressing field:

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\mapsto\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}^{\varphi}}\vcentcolon=(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varphi})^{*}\phi=(\boldsymbol{u}\circ\varphi)^{*}\phi=\varphi^{*}({\boldsymbol{u}}^{*}\phi)=\varphi^{*}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}.

(175)

Seen another way,
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
is a tensor field on
N
, therefore
\Diff(N)
acts on it naturally via pullback
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\mapsto(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\varphi}\vcentcolon=\varphi^{*}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
. Either way, this means that the space
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
of dressed fields is fibered by the right action of
\Diff(N)
:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\times\Diff(N)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},

(176)

\displaystyle(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\varphi)

\displaystyle\mapsto R_{\varphi}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}:=\varphi^{*}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},

it is thus a principal bundle
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\xrightarrow{\pi}\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}/\Diff(N)=\vcentcolon\mathcal{M}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
. From there, the bundle geometry of
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
parallels that of
\Phi
described in previous sections. Its associated SES of groups is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Diff}(N)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Aut}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\mathcal{M}^{\boldsymbol{u}}),

(177)

where
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(N):=\big\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}:\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\rightarrow\Diff(N)\,|\,R^{\star}_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\varphi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\varphi\big\}

is the gauge group of
\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
. The latter being a subgroup of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\Diff(N)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
, acting on
\Gamma(T\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
and
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
the usual way. In particular, we may immediately write that, for
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\Diff(N)\big)
, a (dressed) form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}};\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\big)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
transforms as:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\big(\!\wedge^{\bullet}\!(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}};\,(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\big),

(178)

\displaystyle\text{with}\qquad(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}+\mathfrak{L}_{\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}\}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\big).

(179)

In exact replica of (
43
) and (
66
) on the original field space
\Phi
. From above, or first principles, one can also write:

(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=R^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\boldsymbol{\alpha^{u}}+\iota_{\{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}\}^{v}}\boldsymbol{\alpha^{u}}
.

Affine changes of flat connection on field space.

As observed in section
3.1.1
, a dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}
supplies a flat connection on
\Phi
,
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\vcentcolon=-\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}
. A second dressing field related to the first by the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\Diff(N)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
(or
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(N)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
as a special case),
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
, induce another flat connection related to the first as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\vcentcolon=-\boldsymbol{du}^{\prime}\circ{\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}}^{-1}=-\boldsymbol{d}(\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi})\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}=-\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1})\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}.

(180)

One must first observe that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\vcentcolon=-\boldsymbol{u}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1})\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}\,\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{ten}}(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M))
. Indeed,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
being seen as
\phi
-dependent,
\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\Gamma(TN)
is a vector field near the identity in
\Diff(N)
. So
\boldsymbol{u}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1})\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}\in\Gamma(TM)
is the
\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}
-related vector field on
M
(remembering (
7
)) near the identity in
\Diff(M)
.
So
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\in\Omega^{1}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
.

Now, since by definition the
\phi
-dependence of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is via
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
, one has that
R_{\psi}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}
for
\psi\in\Diff(M)
– which also secures the fact that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{u}^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}
remains a well-defined dressing field. Which is, at the linear level,
\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{d\varphi}=0
, for
X^{v}\in\Gamma(V\Phi)
and
X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)
. This implies that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}(X^{v})=0
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{hor}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
. This also allows to work out that the equivariance is

\displaystyle R_{\psi}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}=-R_{\psi}^{\star}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1})\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}\big)=-(\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u})_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1})\circ(\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}=\psi^{-1}_{*}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\circ\psi,

(181)

as one would expect.
So indeed
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\mathfrak{diff}(M)\big)
.
Thus, as per the discussion of Ehresmann connections in section
2.4.1
:

\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}\in\mathcal{C}
.
Said otherwise, the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\Diff(N)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
– or
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(N)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Aut}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
as a special case – on the space of dressing fields induces affine changes of flat connections (
180
).

One may then notice that, using (
2.1.1
), (
179
) can be rewritten as a relation between (basic) forms on
\Phi
:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}-\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{-\boldsymbol{u}_{*}[\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\circ\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}]\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}\phi\big),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}-\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}}\phi\big).

(182)

Therefore, for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha(\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi)\,\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
, one has that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}-\alpha\big(\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}}\phi;\phi\big),

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}-\iota_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\text{\tiny$0$}}^{v}}\ \boldsymbol{\alpha},\quad\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{basic}}(\Phi).

(183)

These might be compared to (
83
)-(
2.4.1
) which reflects the ambiguity in the construction of basic forms via the horizontalization procedure performed using Ehresmann connections, discussed in section
2.4.1
.

This discussion reinforced the observation made in section
3.1.1
above, showing that, on
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
, using the DFM and Ehresmann connections to build a basic counterpart
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{b}\in\Omega^{1}_{\text{inv}}(\Phi)
indeed give formally very analogous result, down to the “ambiguity” arising in both approaches. A key difference being that, when using Ehresmann connections, the ambiguity is an affine shift in
\mathcal{C}
that cannot be in general ascribed to the action of a group, while it is so (a priori) when using the DFM.

It must be observed that
(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\varphi}
is
\Diff(M)
-invariant for all
\varphi\in\Diff(N)
,
so any representative in the
\Diff(N)
-orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\Diff(N)}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}]
of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
is as good a coordinatisation of
[\phi]\in\mathcal{M}
as any other. In other words we have, a priori,
\mathcal{O}_{\Diff(N)}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}]\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\Diff(M)}[\phi]
, that is
\mathcal{M}^{\boldsymbol{u}}\simeq\mathcal{M}
.
Which in turn implies that the “physical symmetries” are to be found in
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\mathcal{M}^{\boldsymbol{u}})\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}(\mathcal{M})
. Given the SES (
177
), it is clear that
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(N)\simeq\Aut_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
is isomorphic to the original gauge group
\boldsymbol{\Diff}(M)\simeq\Aut_{v}(\Phi)
– and by extension
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
.
The new symmetry
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})\supset\Aut_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
a priori does not enjoy a more direct physical interpretation than the original it replaces.
This suggests that the terminology “physical symmetry” often encountered in the literature to describe transformations like (
174
), (
176
) and (
179
) is essentially misleading
– these are also often called “surface symmetries” or “corner symmetries” in the literature on the covariant phase space approach to field theory, which will be discussed in sections
4
and
.

One may wonder why bother to apply the DFM if one ends up with an equivalent gauge symmetry. The point is that a dressing field may simply be available in a theory as a matter of fact, and its existence have implications for the formal properties and interpretation of the theory that one may as well be aware of. Such would be the case e.g. if part of the symmetry is reducible, as discussed in e.g.
3.2.1
, and therefore
artificial
: The theory is then trimmed from its superfluous formal structure, down to its most economical (“Ockamized”) and essential version.

Furthermore, it may be that the constructive process by which one obtain a dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
from existing fields of the theory is such that the ambiguity reflected in the a priori relation (
174
) is minimal, so that in effect
\varphi
’s form a subgroup of
\Diff(N)
, perhaps even a discrete one (the best possible situation would be that this subgroup is the trivial group).
Clearly, this possibility is forfeited if a dressing field is introduced by hand in a theory – as is often the case in the edge mode literature.
We further develop the discussion of these points in section
3.4
.

3.3
Dressed regions and integrals

As observed in section
2.5.2
, for
U\in\boldsymbol{U}(M)
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)
,
integrals
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
are objects on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
(with values in
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)
). Integrals that are invariant under the action of
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
as defined by (
133
) (i.e. integrals of tensorial integrand) are well-defined on the associated bundle of regions
\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)\vcentcolon=\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)/\sim
, quotient of the product space by the action of
\Diff(M)
(
104
): They have well-defined projection along
\bar{\pi}:\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)\rightarrow\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)
,
(\phi,U)\mapsto[\phi,U]=[\psi^{*}\phi,\psi^{-1}(U)]
, and can be said “basic” w.r.t.
\bar{\pi}
.

In section
3.1
, we defined dressed objects as being in Im
\,\pi^{\star}
, i.e. basic on
\Phi
, with the projection realised via a dressing field,
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}\sim\pi
.
And relying on the formal similarity of the actions of
F_{\boldsymbol{u}}
and
\Xi\in\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
, the rule of thumb to obtain the dressing
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}
of a form
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is to replace the field-dependent parameter
\boldsymbol{\psi}
in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\Xi^{\star}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
by the dressing field
\boldsymbol{u}
.

In the same way, we define dressed integrals as being basic on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
, i.e. in Im
\,\bar{\pi}^{\star}
, with the projection realised as:

\begin{split}\bar{F}_{\boldsymbol{u}}:\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)&\rightarrow\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)\simeq\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\times\boldsymbol{U}(N),\\
(\phi,U)&\mapsto\bar{F}_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi,U)\vcentcolon=\big(F_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\phi),\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\big)=\big(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\big).\end{split}

(184)

We highlight the fact that the region
U^{\boldsymbol{u}}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\in\boldsymbol{U}(N)
is a map
U^{\boldsymbol{u}}:\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{U}(N)
s.t. for
\psi\in\Diff(M)
:

\displaystyle(U^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\psi}\vcentcolon=\bar{R}^{\star}_{\psi}\,U^{\boldsymbol{u}}=(R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\circ\psi^{-1}(U)=(\psi^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{u})^{-1}\circ\psi^{-1}(U)=\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)=\vcentcolon U^{\boldsymbol{u}}.

(185)

The same then holds for
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
:
(U^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\vcentcolon=\bar{\Xi}^{\star}U^{\boldsymbol{u}}=U^{\boldsymbol{u}}
.
Therefore,
U^{\boldsymbol{u}}
is a
\phi
-dependent
\Diff(M)
-invariant region of
true spacetime
. It is indeed a key insight of GR that, by the conjunction of the hole argument and point-coincidence argument
[
Norton1993
;
Stachel2014
;
Giovanelli2021
]
, spacetime is defined in a
relational
and
\Diff(M)
-invariant way by its physical field content. This fact is tacitly encoded by the
\Diff(M)
-covariance of general relativistic theories, it is made manifest by the DFM:
U^{\boldsymbol{u}}
are manifestly
\Diff(M)
-invariant
\phi
-
relationally
defined regions of the physical spacetime – on which the relationally defined
\Diff(M)
-invariant fields
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\phi
live, and are to be integrated over.

Let us further observe that, as a consequence, the (physical/relational) boundary
\partial U^{\boldsymbol{u}}
of a physical spacetime region is of necessity
\Diff(M)
-invariant. Thus, the often encountered claim that “boundaries in general relativistic physics break
\Diff(M)
-invariance” – and that the “edge modes” d.o.f. sometimes introduced as a consequence are the Goldstone bosons associated to that symmetry breaking – is deeply misguided. It is logically equivalent to the hole argument that temporarily confused Einstein about
\Diff(M)
, and overlooks the resolution stemming from the key insight of his point coincidence argument: relationality.

So, on the space
\Omega^{\bullet}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
we define:

\begin{split}\tilde{F}_{\boldsymbol{u}}:\Omega^{\bullet}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)&\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{\text{basic}}}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(N)\big)\times\boldsymbol{U}(N)\\
(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)&\mapsto\tilde{F}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)\vcentcolon=\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\big).\end{split}

(186)

With indeed
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{\text{basic}}}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(N)\big)\simeq\Omega^{\bullet}\big(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\Omega^{\text{top}}(N)\big)
, as dressed fields
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
live on
N
.
Then, in formal analogy with (
133
), the dressing of an integral
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}\vcentcolon=\langle\boldsymbol{\omega},U\rangle=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is:

\begin{split}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}})^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\langle\ ,\ \rangle\circ\tilde{F}_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha},U)=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\int_{\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)}\hskip-11.38109pt\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}}.\end{split}

(187)

We remark that the residual symmetry
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}},\Diff(N)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
may then potentially act on dressed integrals, analogously to (
133
), as:

\displaystyle\left(({\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}})^{\boldsymbol{u}}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=\int_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{-1}(U^{\boldsymbol{u}})}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}})^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}.

(188)

Now, for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)
one has:

\begin{split}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U},\end{split}

(189)

by the invariance property (
128
) of the integration pairing.
Therefore,
{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{U}=\langle\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle=\boldsymbol{d}\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{*}\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)\rangle=\boldsymbol{d}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{U}}^{\boldsymbol{u}})
. The latter can also be proven in a way entirely analogous to (
125
) using the lemma (
156
)-(
157
) and concluding by the invariance property (
129
). This calculation also supplies the analogue of (
126
)/(
137
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{(}\boldsymbol{d\alpha}_{U})^{\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d\alpha}_{U}+\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle,

(190)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},U\rangle^{\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{d\alpha},U\rangle+\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{du}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}\boldsymbol{\alpha},U\rangle.

This is relevant to understand the interplay between DFM and the variational principle.

Indeed, consider a Lagrangian
L\in\Omega^{0}_{\text{tens}}\big(\Phi,\Omega^{\text{top}}(U)\big)
with action functional
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
:

S=\langle L,U\rangle=\int_{U}L
.
We have the dressed Lagrangian
L^{\boldsymbol{u}}=L(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
, which is a manifestly
relational
rewriting of the bare theory in term of the
\Diff(M)
-invariant relational variables
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
.
The corresponding dressed action is

S^{\boldsymbol{u}}=\langle L^{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}(U)=\langle L,U\rangle=S
.
Thus, on the one hand the variational principle is
\boldsymbol{d}S=0=\boldsymbol{d}(S^{\boldsymbol{u}})
, and on the other hand we have the identity, analogue of (
138
):

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{d}S)^{\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}S+\langle\mathfrak{L}_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}L,U\rangle

(191)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}S+\langle d\iota_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}L,U\rangle=\boldsymbol{d}S+\langle\iota_{\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{u}\circ\boldsymbol{u}^{-1}}L,\partial U\rangle.

The fact that
\boldsymbol{d}S
and its dressing differ by a boundary term implies that the variational principles for the undressed and dressed fields are equivalent, and that the space
S^{\boldsymbol{u}}
of dressed solutions is isomorphic to the space
S
of solutions of the undressed theory.

This is fundamental to the relational interpretation of the DFM: it implies that the field equations satisfied by the physical/relational variables
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}
are functionally the same as those satisfied by the gauge variables
\phi
. This will be shown explicitly in section
. Already we stress the fact that it clarifies the reason why, e.g., GR is applied successfully when confronted to physical observation even though the standard formulation uses in principle non-observable
\Diff(M)
-variables (such as the metric field
\phi_{1}=g_{\mu\nu}
):
What we actually confront to observations is the dressed/relational theory
L^{\boldsymbol{u}}=L(\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}})
– often inaccurately called a “gauge-fixed” version of the theory – where coordinatisation is made relationally, w.r.t. other fields (other d.o.f.
\phi_{2}
than the gravitational ones: the laboratories, earth, the solar system, the local group, etc.), which is formally identical to the bare version.
So, the fact that it is possible to successfully test GR without explicitly solving first the issue of its observables is precisely due to its relational character, as encoded by its
\Diff(M)
-equivariance, which technically implies that its bare and dressed/physical version are functionally identical.

3.4
Discussion

Let us concisely sum up the main technical idea of this section, before offering some more conceptual comments.
The DFM is a systematic and formal way to obtain objects, built from fields
\phi
and their variations
\boldsymbol{d}\phi
, that are (partially or fully) invariant under diffeomorphisms of
M
:
basic
forms on field space
\Phi
, in the bundle theoretic terminology.
It is a
conditional
proposition:
If
one can find/build a dressing field,
then
it gives the algorithm to produce these invariants (and analyse possible residual symmetries).

We must observe that the existence of global dressing fields are not guaranteed, global being meant in two ways.
Firstly, it is clear that
\phi
-independent dressing fields of the type
\mathcal{D}r[\mathbb{R}^{n},M]
, i.e.
u:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow M
, are nothing but
global
coordinate charts, which may not exist depending on the topology of
M
. Topological obstructions remain relevant in the
\phi
-dependent case
\boldsymbol{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow M
, as they may imply that no field
\phi
is globally defined, or non-vanishing, everywhere on
M
and fit to serve as a global coordinatisation.
25
25

25

One may e.g. think of the Poincaré–Hopf theorem (or its special case, the Hairy Ball theorem) stating that there is no nowhere vanishing vector fields on compact manifold with vanishing Euler characteristic. Admittedly, this particular case may be of limited (fundamental) physical importance, as spacetime is modelled by Lorentzian 4-dimensional manifolds requiring precisely the existence of such a nowhere vanishing vector field – hence spacetime is either non-compact or has vanishing Euler characteristic. See
[
ONeill1983
]
p.149.

Secondly, as noted in section
3.1.1
, dressing fields globally defined
on field space

\Phi
provide flat Ehresmann connections, which in turn imply the global triviality of
\Phi
as a bundle. A priori, if
\Phi
is globally non-trivial, dressing fields would only be available locally, over open subsets
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
(being compatible with the local triviality of
\Phi
).

Remark that the formalism extends familiar notions of standard differential geometry. Indeed, as just noted,
\phi
-independent dressings
\boldsymbol{u}\rightarrow u\in\mathcal{D}r[\mathbb{R}^{n},U\subset M]
are just coordinates charts of
M
(thus non-variational objects,
\boldsymbol{d}u=0
). In that case, dressed variables
\phi^{u}
are the usual coordinate representations of the fields
\phi
, while the formula defining dressed integrals (
187
) reproduces the
definition
of integration on
M
– the general case of (
187
) may thus be understood as defining
integration on spacetime
.
Furthermore,
\Diff(N)=\Diff(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is then just the group of transition functions of
M
(i.e. coordinate changes), so that relations (
176
), (
179
), (
178
) and (
188
) reduce to standard coordinate change formulas for fields, field-dependent volume forms, and integrals.

Explicit in the DFM, is the proposition that the dressing field is to be found among, or built from, the existing set of fields
\phi
of a theory: it is key to the natural relational interpretation of the formalism. This is the situation of most fundamental physical significance for the analysis of a
\Diff(M)
-theory.

It is not so for dressing fields introduced by
fiat
as d.o.f. independent from the original field space
\Phi
, i.e. admitting
\boldsymbol{d}u\neq 0
: It amounts to extending the original field space to
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi+u
, and thus to considering an altogether different theoretical framework where both the kinematics and the
character
of the symmetry are altered.
Two common situations are covered by this case of the DFM.

The first is when starting from a
\Diff(M)
-theory on
\Phi
, and one tries to reduce the symmetry by introducing such an
ad hoc
dressing field. When
\Diff(M)
is seemingly broken by a background structure, like a non-relational boundary, the move is often understood as “restoring
\Diff(M)
-invariance”. This is typically the premise of the literature on “edge modes”, see e.g.
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
;
Speranza2018
;
Speranza2022
;
Ciambelli2023
]
.
An immediate drawback of such a move was pointed out earlier, in section
3.2.2
: The original
\Diff(M)
symmetry is replaced by an isomorphic, and a priori no more physical,
\Diff(N)
symmetry – think again of the case
N=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
u\in\mathcal{D}r[\mathbb{R}^{n},U\subset M]
.
Clearly, there is little chance for
\Diff(N)
to be a symmetry giving relevant
new
information about the theory under consideration (not already given by
\Diff(M)
).
And the
ad hoc
dressing field being introduced non-constructively, there is no compelling argument for ever having
\Diff(N)
reduced to a subgroup (small or discrete) either. Therefore, all “issues” one had with
\Diff(M)
one has now with
\Diff(N)
, defeating the purpose of introducing a dressing field in the first place.

The second situation is when starting from a theory devoid of
\Diff(M)
symmetry, and one tries to enforce it via the introduction of an
ad hoc
dressing field. In that context,
ad hoc
dressing fields are known as (gravitational) Stueckelberg fields, and the move is again seen as “restoring
\Diff(M)
-invariance”. This is typically the case of the literature on massive gravity, bi-gravity, and sometimes string theory
[
deRham2014
;
Green-Thorn1991
;
Siegel1994
]
.
Such an artificially enforced
\Diff(M)
symmetry have no physical content, and is there mainly to hide background non-dynamical structures – often some reference metric (preserved by a subgroup of
\Diff(M)
, which is the real symmetry of the theory).
For that reason these
\Diff(M)
symmetries introduced via
ad hoc
dressing fields, i.e. Stueckelberg fields, are called “
artificial
” in the philosophy of physics literature
[
Pitts2005
;
Pitts2008
;
Pitts2009
;
Pitts2012
]
.
26
26

26

Indeed, enforcing a symmetry via a Stueckelberg trick, is an instance of “Kretschmannisation”: rewriting a theory so as to display a strictly formal symmetry without physical signature or content. The name stems from the famous “Kretschmann objection” against general covariance as a fundamental feature of GR, according to which any theory can be made
\Diff(M)
-invariant, or generally covariant. Analysis of the objection resulted in the realisation that one must distinguish between
substantive

\Diff(M)
/general covariance as a distinctive native feature of a theory, with physical content/signature, from
artificial

\Diff(M)
/general covariance which is implemented by hand (often via the introduction of extra structures/fields, e.g. à la Stueckelberg) and thus without physical content. A comparable crucial distinction exists for (internal) gauge symmetries, in response to a “generalised Kretschmann objection” to the gauge principle
[
Pitts2009
;
Francois2018
;
Berghofer-et-al2023
]
: There, a key physical signature of substantive gauge symmetries is the trade-off between gauge invariance and locality of a theory, absent for artificial gauge symmetries.

The class of theories displaying such artificial
\Diff(M)
symmetries is thus “non-general-relativistic” in a deep sense: They are only superficially and formally alike GR, but betray its key physical insight, i.e. the relationality and absence of background structures/fields that a substantive
\Diff(M)
symmetry encodes.

Field-dependent dressing fields
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)
, whose d.o.f. are extracted from the original field space
\Phi
, are much more interesting.
They allow in principle a formal implementation of a
relational description
of the physics of general relativistic field theory.
As already noted early in section
3.1
, the invariant dressed fields
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}
defined by (
3.1
)
are readily understood as a relational description of the physical d.o.f., in that
the d.o.f. of the fields
\phi
are
coordinatised relative to each other
.
As a result, only
\Diff(M)
-invariant, physical relational d.o.f. are manifest.
27
27

27

Said otherwise, by dressing
\phi\mapsto\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}(\phi)}
, one reshuffles the d.o.f. in presence so as to exhibits only the relational and physical ones.

It has long been advocated that the (completely) relational character of general relativistic physics is indeed one of its key innovating feature, tacitly encoded by the
\Diff(M)
covariance – as understood by the conjunction the hole and point-coincidence arguments.
See e.g.
[
Norton1993
;
Rovelli2002
;
Rovelli2004
;
Tamborino2012
;
Stachel2014
;
Rovelli2014
;
Giovanelli2021
]
. The DFM can be understood as a way to reformulate a general relativistic field theory in a
\Diff(M)
-invariant and manifestly relational way.
Accordingly, one may suggest to rebrand General Relativity as “
General Relationality
”. We shall here tacitly adopt this nomenclature shift whenever writing “GR”.

Relatedly, as observed in section
3.2.2
, only by
constructively
producing a
\phi
-dependent dressing field is there any chance for the “new” gauge symmetry
\Diff(N)
to be reduced to a small, discrete, or trivial subgroup. In concrete situations, it may be reduced to a discrete choice: that of reference coordinatizing field among the collection
\phi
.

Finally, we draw attention on the fact that the DFM as developed here is the common geometric framework underpinning a number of notions appearing in recent years in the literature on gravity, or
\Diff(M)
-theories more generally: the already mentioned massive gravity and bi-gravity theories
[
deRham2014
]
, but also the notion of “gravitational dressings” as proposed in
[
Giddings-Donnelly2016
;
Giddings-Donnelly2016-bis
;
Giddings-Kinsella2018
;
Giddings2019
;
Giddings-Weinberg2020
]
, or yet that of “dynamical reference frames” as proposed in
[
carrozza-et-al2022
;
Hoehn-et-al2022
]
.
Notably, dressing fields underlie the notions of “
gravitational edge modes
” as introduced by
[
DonnellyFreidel2016
]
in the covariant phase space approach to the presymplectic structure of gravity over bounded regions, which spurred some amount of activity since its inception – e.g.
[
Geiller2017
;
Speranza2018
;
Geiller2018
;
Chandrasekaran_Speranza2021
]
or
[
Freidel-et-al2020-1
;
Freidel-et-al2020-2
;
Freidel-et-al2020-3
]
,
as well as the derived and closely related notion of “embedding fields”
[
Speranza2019
;
Freidel:2021
;
Ciambelli-et-al2022
;
Speranza2022
;
Ciambelli2023
]
– see also
[
Kabel-Wieland2022
]
.
The following sections
4
and
, will substantiate this claim.
In addition to the technical streamlining stemming from the DFM, the insights it offers and conceptual clarifications just discussed should be kept in mind when looking at the cited literature through the DFM lense.

4
Covariant phase space methods and bundle geometry

The covariant phase space approach was introduced to study the (pre) symplectic structure of gauge field theory. As (re-) introduced by
[
Zuckerman1986
;
CrnkovicWitten1986
;
Crnkovic1987
]
, the aim was
to associate a symplectic structure to a gauge field theory given by a Lagrangian
L
over some region
U\subset M
, and so doing while keeping spacetime symmetries manifest (with in mind a possible covariant canonical or geometric quantization). Nowadays, it is mainly used to derive charges as physically interesting quantities, realising the symmetries of the theory through their poisson algebra: it finds notable applications in the study of asymptotic symmetries and gravitational waves physics, as well as in the study of bounded subsystems and their symmetries (like black holes).
28
28

28

In this case, Noether charges capture e.g. the Hawking-Finkelstein entropy
[
Wald1993
]
, or the Komar mass (see
[
Choquet-Bruhat2009
]
def. 4.6, eq. (4.8), p.460).

Classical references are
[
Lee-Wald1990
;
Ashtekar-et-al1990
]
, and modern introductions are
[
Compere-Fiorucci2018
;
Harlow-Wu2020
]
(see also
[
Farajollahi-Luckock2002
]
for a compact summary).

Its roots go further back though. The recent review
[
Gieres2021
]
gives historical context and shows the relation of covariant phase space methods to other approaches such as the multisymplectic formalism – about which we recommend
[
Helein2012
]
– and the variational bicomplex
[
Anderson1992
]
.

In what follows we propose our own account of the topic, articulating covariant phase space approach for
\Diff(M)
-theories with the bundle geometry of field space
\Phi
as described in section
2
. Our main goal in so doing, is both to streamline the derivation of key results – such as charges for both field-independent and field-dependent gauge parameter, and their Poisson bracket – and provide clear understanding of new ones, such as the vertical/gauge transformations of the symplectic potential and 2-form, that are necessary to apply immediately the DFM and thus give the general form of the
basic presymplectic structure
of a theory (encompassing existing constructions, e.g. “edge mode extended phase space”).

4.1
Covariant phase space for
{\Diff(M)}
-theories

Consider a theory given by the Lagrangian
n
-form
L^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\,dx^{n}\in\Omega^{0}(\Phi)
,
n=
dim
M
, s.t.:

\displaystyle L^{\prime}=L+d\ell:\Phi

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{n}(M),

(192)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto L^{\prime}(\phi).

The two Lagrangians
L^{\prime}
and
L
belong to the same (
n^{\text{th}}
) DeRham cohomology group of
M
.
The exact term
\ell
is called a boundary Lagrangian, as over a region
U\in\boldsymbol{U}(M)
with boundary
\partial U
, the associated action functional

S^{\prime}=\langle L^{\prime},U\rangle=\int_{U}L^{\prime}
is:
S^{\prime}=S+\langle\ell,\partial U\rangle=S+\int_{\partial U}\ell
.
The variational principle, finding extrema of the action
\boldsymbol{d}S^{\prime}=\langle\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime},U\rangle\equiv 0
, requires to consider the 1-form
\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi)
, which is generically s.t.:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{d}L+d\boldsymbol{d}\ell=\boldsymbol{E}+d\big(\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{d}\ell\big)=\boldsymbol{E}+d\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime},

(193)

where
\boldsymbol{E}=E(\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi)
are the field equations 1-form, and
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(\prime)}=\theta^{(\prime)}(\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi)
is the presymplectic potential current.
We see that both
L^{\prime}
and
L
have the same field equations but distinct presymplectic potential currents,
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}
: this is what a boundary term/Lagrangian does, it contributes to the presymplectic structure by shifting the potential.
The presymplectic 2-form current of the theory is defined by
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\vcentcolon=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\theta}\,\in\Omega^{2}(\Phi)
, i.e.
\Theta\big(\boldsymbol{d}\phi\wedge\boldsymbol{d}\phi;\phi\big)
. It is the same for both
L^{\prime}
and
L
, it is thus cohomologically well defined.
Given a codimension 1 submanifold
\Sigma
of
U\subset M
, the presymplectic potential and presymplectic 2-form are objects on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Phi)\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
, so on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\Sigma)=\langle\boldsymbol{\theta},\Sigma\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\theta},

(194)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\Theta},\Sigma)=\langle\boldsymbol{\Theta},\Sigma\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Theta}.

(195)

\displaystyle\,=\boldsymbol{d}\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\Sigma)=\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\theta},\Sigma)

The configuration space is the space of solutions
\mathcal{S}\vcentcolon=\{\phi\in\Phi\,|\,\boldsymbol{E}_{|\phi}=0\}
, a subbundle of
\Phi
whose base
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{S}/\Diff(M)
is the reduced phase space. We remark that there is then a reduced version of the associated bundle of regions:
\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)_{|\mathcal{S}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{S}\times_{\text{{\tiny$\Diff(M)$}}}\boldsymbol{U}(M)=\mathcal{S}\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)/\sim
. On-shell integrated forms are objects on
\mathcal{S}\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
, and those that are invariant under the induced action of
\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)\simeq C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)
are well-defined on
\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)_{|\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
– as discussed in section
2.5.2
.

One original motivation of the formalism was to associate a symplectic space to the gauge theory
L^{\prime}
over
U\subset M
:
for this
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}
must be well-defined on
\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}(M)_{|\mathcal{S}}
. This would happens if
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is tensorial or basic, see again section
2.5.2
, or if its transformation is a boundary term and either
\partial\Sigma=\emptyset
or boundary conditions (b.c.) are given.
The trouble is, generically none of this occurs. In particular, when
\partial\Sigma\neq\emptyset
and no reasonable b.c. can be imposed,
we have an occurrence of “boundary problem”.

Another instance of “boundary problem” arises when, studying the symmetries of a field theory, one establishes the relation between Noether charges and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}
:
Indeed generically, the Noether charges fails to be Hamiltonian generator of the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
on
\Phi
(
X^{v}
are not Hamiltonian vector fields) due to boundary contributions that cannot be “integrated” into a redefinition of the charges (an issue typical of “open” systems).
Said otherwise, Noether charges fails to be a moment map for
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
.

To establish such boundary problems in their most generic form, in as clear a way as possible, in the following our goals are:
First, to derive an expression for the Noether charges associated to the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
and exhibit their relation to
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}
. This will allow us to also address the question of the definition of their Poisson bracket.

Second, we will derive the vertical/gauge transformations – a.k.a. field-dependent gauge transformations – of all objects in presence,
L^{(\prime)}
,
\boldsymbol{d}L^{(\prime)}
,
\boldsymbol{E}
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(\prime)}
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
: This will both highlight the general form of the first boundary problem (the “non-basicity” of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}
), and help to give its formal solution in the form of the dressed/basic presymplectic structure obtained via the DFM (applying its simple rule of thumb).

For this tasks, we will need the equivariance and verticality properties of these objects:
Since they are all are form on
\Phi
with values in
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
, their
\Diff(M)
-equivariance is given by the pullback representation, and their
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
-equivariance given by the Lie derivative representation. So, for
\psi\in\Diff(M)
and
X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)\simeq\Gamma(TM)
:

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}L^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}L^{\prime}\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}L^{\prime}=\mathfrak{L}_{X}L^{\prime}=\vcentcolon\alpha(X;\phi),

(196)

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{d}\alpha(X;\phi),

(197)

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\boldsymbol{E}\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{E}=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{E},

(198)

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X),

(199)

\displaystyle R^{\star}_{\psi}\boldsymbol{\Theta}

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\qquad\text{so}\qquad\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X),

(200)

The term
\alpha(X;\phi)
can be called the Lagrangian anomaly, a 0-form on
\Phi
linear in
X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)
, while
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X)
may be called the “symplectic anomaly”, a 1-form on
\Phi
linear in
X
. Given (
193
), the two are related by:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\alpha(X;\phi)=d\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X)+\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{E}=d\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X)+\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{E}\big),

(201)

where we used the fact that
d\boldsymbol{E}=0
since
\boldsymbol{E}
is a top form on
M
.

We may notice already that, on account of (
200
), a priori
\Diff(M)
does not act as symplectomorphisms. So we should expect obstruction from
X^{v}\in\Gamma(V\Phi)
being Hamiltonian vector fields for a moment map/Noether charge.

Finally, the
C^{\infty}\big(\Phi,\Diff(M)\big)\simeq\boldsymbol{\Diff}_{v}(\Phi)
-transformations of the integrals of the above objects will be obtained using (
133
) from section
2.5.2
.

4.1.1
Noether charges for field-independent gauge parameters

Let us proceed anyway to find the expression of Noether charges for the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
.
As we’ve already established in section
2.2.2
(just below (
50
)), and reiterated just above, the
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
-equivariance of the Lagrangian as a 0-form on
\Phi
is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}L^{\prime}=\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{L}_{X}L^{\prime}=d(\iota_{X}L^{\prime})=\vcentcolon d\beta(X,\phi)=\alpha(X;\phi),

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{L}_{X}L+\mathfrak{L}_{X}\,d\ell=d\big(\iota_{X}L+\mathfrak{L}_{X}\ell\big)=\vcentcolon d\big(\iota_{X}L+\beta_{\ell}(X,\phi)\big).

(202)

Where we used the fact that
n
-forms on
M
are
d
-closed.
From this and (
193
) we get:

-\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{E}=d\big(\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\beta(X,\phi)\big)
. Which tells us that there is an on-shell (
\boldsymbol{E}=0
)
d
-closed quantity, the Noether
(n-1)
-form current:

\displaystyle J(X;\phi)\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\beta(X,\phi)-d\gamma(X;\phi),

(203)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\iota_{X}L-d\gamma(X;\phi),\quad\ \in\,\Omega^{n-1}(M).

By definition then,
-\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{E}=dJ(X,\phi)
.
The current is independent of the boundary Lagrangian
\ell
, so depends only on the DeRham cohomology class
[L^{\prime}]=[L]
, but still defined a priori up to a
d
-exact term
d\gamma(X;\phi)
(the sign is conventional).
Given a codimension 1 submanifold
\Sigma\subset U\in\boldsymbol{U}(M)
, the Noether charge is defined on
\Phi\times\boldsymbol{U}(M)
as:

\displaystyle Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)\vcentcolon=\langle J(X;\phi),\Sigma\rangle=\int_{\Sigma}J(X;\phi)=\int_{\Sigma}\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\iota_{X}L-d\gamma(X;\phi).

(204)

Notice this can be seen as a map

\displaystyle Q_{\Sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}X};\ \,):\Phi

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{diff}(M)^{*},

(205)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto Q_{\Sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}X};\phi).

where
\mathfrak{diff}(M)^{*}
is the dual of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
, as indeed
Q_{\Sigma}(\ \,;\phi):\mathfrak{diff}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
X\mapsto Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)
, is a linear map.

Now, in the spirit of the covariant phase space, all quantities ultimately must be expressed on-shell. So, throughout, we will endeavor to find expressions of our key objects in terms of the field equations. Case in point, using (
) derived in
(from the assumptions that we are dealing with the field space of a gauge theory given by a first-order Lagrangian), we find that the Noether current and charge are:

\displaystyle J(X;\phi)

\displaystyle=d\big(\theta(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi)-\gamma(X;\phi)\big)\,-\,E(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi),

(206)

\displaystyle Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}\theta(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi)-\gamma(X;\phi)\,-\int_{\Sigma}E(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi).

(207)

The charge is manifestly a boundary term on-shell.
This result is consistent with the expectation that, the current being
d
-closed on-shell by construction, and assuming the region
U
has trivial topology, it should indeed be
d
-exact. The fact is made manifest by the above forms.
29
29

29

Remark that had we postulated that there is another
d
-exact contribution to the
\Diff(M)
-anomaly (
4.1.1
),
\alpha_{\gamma}(X;\phi)=d\beta_{\gamma}(X;\phi)
, the current would have been
J(X;\phi)=d\big(\theta(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi)-\gamma(X;\phi)\big)-\beta_{\gamma}(X;\phi)-E(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi)
. But since by definition
\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{E}=-dJ(X;\phi)
, it follows that on-shell
d\beta_{\gamma}(X;\phi)=0
. So, assuming trivial topology, we get
\beta_{\gamma}(X;\phi)=d\bar{\gamma}(X;\phi)
. A term that can be reabsorbed in the
d
-ambiguity of the current:
\gamma\mapsto\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma+\bar{\gamma}
.

Remark that from (
206
) follows
\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{E}=dE(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi)
.

Then, one must assess the relation of the Noether charge
Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)
to the presymplectic 2-form
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}
, or of
J(X;\phi)
to
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. This will allow to see under which conditions the Noether charge is “integrable” into a Hamiltonian generator for
X^{v}\in\Gamma(V\Phi)
, or in other words, if it defines via (
205
) a moment map for the action of
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
on field space
\Phi
.

The most direct way is to look at the
\mathfrak{diff}(M)
-equivariance of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{L}_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}=\vcentcolon\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X),

(208)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=-\boldsymbol{d}\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X),

\displaystyle\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}=-\boldsymbol{d}\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{d}\beta_{\ell}(X;\phi)+\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X),

It is easy to work out an expression for
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X)
in terms of the field equation:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}(X)\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{L}_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\iota_{X}d\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}+d\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime},

\displaystyle=\iota_{X}(\boldsymbol{d}L^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{E})+d\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime},

\displaystyle=\iota_{X}(\boldsymbol{d}L-\boldsymbol{E})+d\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{d}\beta_{\ell}(X;\phi).

(209)

Inserting this into (
208
), and given the definition of the Noether current (
203
), we finally obtain:

\displaystyle\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{d}J(X;\phi)+d\left(\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{d}\gamma(X;\phi)\right)-\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{E},

(210)

\displaystyle\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{d}Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)+\int_{\partial\Sigma}\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{d}\gamma(X;\phi)\ \,-\int_{\Sigma}\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{E}.

We observe that, even on-shell, there is a boundary obstruction preventing the Noether charge to be a moment map.
The integrand of the boundary term in (
210
) is often called the “flux term” or “symplectic flux”, and is sometimes interpreted as meaning that there is physical flux leaking through the boundary
\partial\Sigma
, making the system “open”.

To “close” the system and reestablish integrability of the Noether charge, one needs to impose boundary condition s.t.
\boldsymbol{d}\gamma(X;\phi)=\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}
, or to restrict the admissible class of diffeomorphisms, considering
X\in\mathfrak{diff}(M)
vanishing at
\partial\Sigma
. An instance of the first approach suggests itself when requiring that the variational principle for
L^{\prime}
be well-posed on a bounded region:
\boldsymbol{d}S^{\prime}=0\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{E}=0
, which necessitates
\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{d}\ell=0_{|\partial U\supset\partial\Sigma}
, in turn implying
\iota_{X}\boldsymbol{\theta}=-\boldsymbol{d}\iota_{X}\ell
. In other words we set
\gamma(X;\phi)=-\iota_{X}\ell
, so that on-shell:

\displaystyle Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Sigma}\theta(\iota_{X}\phi;\phi)+\iota_{X}\ell\ _{|\mathcal{S}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\iota_{X^{v}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\Sigma}=-\boldsymbol{d}Q_{\Sigma}(X;\phi)\ _{|\mathcal{S}}.

(211)

The boundary Lagrangian makes its return into the expression of the current/charge.
This may be compared to Eqs.(2.46)-(2.47)/(2.50) of
[
Harlow-Wu2020
]
, where the presymplectic potential is assumed to decompose as
\boldsymbol{\theta}=-\boldsymbol{d}\ell+d\boldsymbol{C}\,_{|\partial\Sigma}
–
